Mahershala Ali Balances Tenderness and Swagger in Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother
2026-09-25

Mahershala Ali Balances Tenderness and Swagger in Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother

TIME | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Mahershala Ali in Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings No one really wants to talk about religious rage, the complex resentment when you feel your God has abandoned you: We like our faith to be tidy and count-your-blessings clean, not clouded with doubt and fury. In writer-director Bassam Tariq’s action thriller Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother, Mahershala Ali plays Latif, a Houston, Texas, hit man who’s also, ostensibly, a devout Muslim: He strives to be a man of faith even as he’s breaking bones (and more) in the name of Allah. Then his wife dies suddenly, making him a single dad with three kids to care for, teenager Fatiha (Adia), her younger brother Qadir (Jahleel Kamara), and an infant, Aziza, who can’t tolerate baby formula; only her mother’s breast milk will do, and supplies are quickly running out. That’s a workaday problem Latif has got to solve, and prayer takes a backseat to his feelings of betrayal. “You take her from me,” he rages in voiceover, “and you expect me to bow down to you?” A crisis of faith and a desperate search for breast milk: As plot drivers go, that’s a pretty novel combination, but Tariq and Ali pull it off here. The tone is equal parts sacred, profane, and comical. Tariq opens the film with a skull-crushing action sequence in which Latif, dressed in swirling robes, beats the heck out of a baddie in a public bathroom; the sequence is exhilarating and brutal, not least because Ali, wrapped in quasi-Samurai warrior garb and wielding a menacing knife, makes such a dashing, beguiling figure. When his wife dies, he realizes his priorities must shift toward his family, but he can’t free himself from his longtime boss Mike (Laith Nakli), who has a special job for him: Mike’s porn-king nephew Hatty (Abubakr Ali) is being harassed by a (Christian) religious fanatic, Pastor Hwan Yoon (John Cho). Can Tariq take care of that? He can—but meanwhile, baby Aziza is yowling with hunger, and it turns out that a sex worker formerly in Hatty’s stable, Tiffany Boone’s Fugazi, is lactating and has a bountiful supply of what baby needs. If Latif’s life of spirituality mingled with bloody violence seemed complicated before, it has now entered the realm of the surreal. Cho and Ali as Pastor Hwan Yoon and Latif Courtesy of Obscured Pictures Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother is most entertaining when it indulges its tawdry side. Hatty, with his tacky sex mansion and bevy of "halal-certified" cuties, is a particularly clever invention, and Cho, decked out in spangled western shirts and sporting a row of gold teeth, appears to be having a blast as a sleazy operator who’s more interested in power than in Jesus. The action sequences are sharp and swift. In his quest for milk, his tiny daughter strapped snugly to his chest, Ali’s Latif is a new kind of action hero. (Some of the movie’s visuals echo the climactic baby-rescue scene of John Woo’s magnificent Hard Boiled, in which Chow Yun Fat descends the wall of a multi-story hospital building while fending off a hail of bullets, even as he cradles a gurgling mite with one arm.) But Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother also tangles with complex issues of faith. It’s clear that Latif’s daughter Fatiha, who breaks fast when she shouldn’t and has a crush on one of the family’s young Mormon neighbors, is straying from the path. But the movie doesn’t judge her for it. The suggestion is that doubt is part of faith—you can’t have the second without the occasional tension of the first—and even Latif will have to face that crisis. The Pakistani-born Tariq previously co-directed (with Omar Mullick) the 2013 documentary These Birds Walk, and he was one of the filmmakers in talks to direct the ill-fated Marvel reboot of Blade, with Ali set to star. In a world where blessings, even mixed ones, are sparse, it’s not a terrible thing that Ali was saved from potentially being chewed up by a Marvel vehicle; he’s a superb actor who deserves better, and Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother gives him plenty to work with. The movie’s title refers to a hadith in which a man approaches the Prophet Muhammad and asks, “To whom should I give the best of me?” The prophet’s answer, so emphatic he repeats it three times, is “Your mother”; only when the man asks the question for a fourth time does the prophet respond, “Your father.” As Latif, Ali captures the spirit of the movie’s title: he seems powered by a contradictory blend of male privilege and humility. Early on, he sees, as if for the first time, the little notes his wife had left all over the house, reminders for herself and the kids of all the minuscule things that need to be taken care of in a household. She had handled all these tasks quietly, and seemingly with ease. Now those things—essentially women’s work—are left to him, including sustaining an infant who needs something he can’t provide. As Latif, always spoiling for a fight, Ali swaggers through the film. But there’s another man inside, a gentle, God-fearing one, and that’s the one you see in the somber beauty of Ali’s eyes. Out of necessity, Latif needs to reconcile with his feminine side. Suddenly, he’s both father and mother, and he finds that filling the role of the latter is the greater challenge. It’s the tenderness he carries within that makes Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother both distinctive and thought provoking. The thrills are just a fringe benefit.

Socialism is More Popular Than Ever in the U.S., According to Polls
2026-09-25

Socialism is More Popular Than Ever in the U.S., According to Polls

TIME | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A person holds a sign reading "capitalism is the crisis, socialism is the solution" as they protest ahead of the G20 Finance ministers and central bank governors meeting in Asheville, North Carolina, on Aug. 28, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings It’s undeniable. Socialism is on the rise—or at least growing in popularity in the U.S. Forty-three percent of Americans have a positive view of socialism, the highest share since Gallup began tracking the measure since 2010. The latest survey, conducted Aug. 3-24, found that socialism has gained ground over time as several market-oriented economic concepts have lost favor. It now ranks ahead of big business, which has fallen to a positive rating of 35% from a peak of 58% in 2012. Capitalism and free enterprise are still more popular than socialism. Capitalism’s positive rating, however, has fallen to 55% from a peak of 61% in 2010 and 2012. Free enterprise is now favored at 77%, compared to its peak of 89% in 2012. Small business remains the most favored economic system at 95%, relatively unchanged in 2010. Those figures, however, mask sharp partisan differences. Democrats view socialism much more favorably than Republicans do, and their views have become more positive over time. This shift has come as progressives and democratic socialists have defeated establishment candidates in several key Democratic primary contests. Zohran Mamdani’s election as New York City Mayor last year marked a breakthrough for the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA). In June, all three of his endorsed candidates won against establishment Democrats in congressional primaries in New York City. Read More: Congress Is Headed for a Massive Turnover Progressives also won high-profile primaries elsewhere, including Senate candidates Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan and Peggy Flanagan in Minnesota, both of whom have not adopted the socialist label, as well as DSA members and House candidates Melat Kiros in Colorado and Chris Rabb in Pennsylvania. Democratic socialist Francesca Hong narrowly lost in the gubernatorial primary in Wisconsin, a battleground state. What Brookings analysts dubbed a “DSA summer” serves to “signal that unhappiness with the Democratic establishment and, in the post-Biden world, opposition to elderly officeholders can be leveraged by young, charismatic DSA candidates—and reflect an impatience with party leadership that transcends ideology.” Republicans have sought to portray those victories as evidence that the Democratic Party is moving too far left ahead of the November midterm elections, while Democratic leaders have tried to temper concerns about the party’s direction. In November 2025, the House voted 285-98 to approve a resolution denouncing the “horrors of socialism,” which was followed by another resolution condemning socialism that passed 220-192 earlier this month. A July analysis by the Cook Political Report found that center-left candidates dominated primaries in the competitive House seats most likely to determine control of Congress, suggesting that progressive victories may not signal a leftward shift across the country. “If left-wing nominees manage to win in swing districts, their overall movement will be strengthened,” the August Brookings analysis said. “If they lose districts deemed winnable and cost Democrats control of the House and Senate, their mainstream presence will likely be diminished.” Still, changing views among the American public could indicate growing openness to what was once a political red line. Read More: 'We're Proving It Works': Inside the DSA’s Push to Remake the Democratic Party Socialism isn’t gaining popularity universally in the U.S. Most of the increase in socialism’s positive rating through 2025 came from growing favorability among Democrats. In 2010, 50% of Democrats viewed socialism positively, according to Gallup. That rose to 66% last year, and remained relatively unchanged at 65% this year. Socialism now matches free enterprise in favorability among Democrats, following a sharp decline in their positive views of free enterprise since 2021. Democrats have also viewed socialism more favorably than capitalism since 2016, when 54% of Democrats viewed capitalism positively, while 60% viewed socialism positively. That year marked a breakthrough for the DSA as longtime Senator Bernie Sanders (I, Vt.) brought the organization to national attention during his presidential run. Sen. Bernie Sanders (I, Vt.) and New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani during a "Get Out The Vote" rally ahead of a primary election at Kings Theater in the Brooklyn borough of New York on June 18, 2026. Adam Gray—Bloomberg/Getty Images Capitalism’s positive rating has further fallen among Democrats, declining from 42% last year to 35%. Other polls have found a similar pattern among Democrats. A CBS/YouGov poll, conducted Aug. 12-14, found that 58% of Democrats viewed socialism positively, compared to 32% for capitalism. College-educated Democrats were especially positive about socialism, with 71% viewing it favorably. Meanwhile, 50% of Democrats viewed capitalism negatively, compared with 18% for socialism. That contrasts with the broader public, which overall favored capitalism to socialism. The poll also found that half of Democrats viewed the label “democratic socialist” positively. A Marquette Law School poll highlighted by CNN in June similarly found that the DSA had a higher net favorability among Democrats and Democrat-leaning independents than Democrats in Congress by 13 points. A national survey conducted by Data for Progress in August 2025 found that Democrats preferred democratic socialism to capitalism by a 58-percentage-point margin. Socialism has also grown in popularity among political independents, accounting for the overall boost to socialism’s favorability in the latest Gallup poll. The share of independents rating socialism positively rose from 37% last year to 45%, surpassing big business which fell to a record low of 32%. Capitalism, free enterprise, and small business still ranked above socialism in favorability among independents. Republicans remained overwhelmingly skeptical of socialism, with just 14% viewing it positively. Large majorities of Republicans continued to have positive views of small business, free enterprise, and capitalism, while 62% of Republicans viewed big business positively. Still, Americans’ economic views are more complicated than the results might initially suggest, according to a Gallup analysis from August. The report, based on open-ended survey responses, found there is broad support for free-market principles like economic competition and market choice, despite concerns about capitalism spurring economic inequality and excessive corporate power. The CBS/YouGov poll, meanwhile, found that support for government intervention crossed party lines, with 75% of Democrats, 62% of independents, and 54% of Republicans supporting government price controls. And the Data for Progress survey showed 70% of 1,257 respondents said the U.S. economic system is “rigged in favor of corporations and the wealthy” and favored replacing it with one that “gives working people a fair shot,” including 67% of independents and 58% of Republicans. Additionally, 83% of respondents, including 81% of independents and 80% of Republicans, agreed that social work and mental health are necessary parts of a public safety budget. Younger Americans are more receptive to socialism, polls show. A demonstrator wears a "SOCIALISM IS THE FUTURE" shirt at a protest in response to two fatal shootings by U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) agents in San Francisco, Calif., on July 14, 2026. Benjamin Fanjoy—Getty Images According to Gallup, 57% of Americans aged 18 to 34 viewed socialism positively, compared with 43% for capitalism. The pattern was reversed among older adults: 65% of those aged 55 and older viewed capitalism positively, while 34% viewed socialism positively. Of Americans aged 35 to 54, 50% rated capitalism positively, while 45% rated socialism positively. The August CBS/YouGov poll also found that younger Americans were more likely to perceive shrinking economic opportunities. Fifty-four percent of American adults under 30 said their opportunities to succeed are worse than their parents’ generation’s, compared with 31% of those aged 65 and older. Of those under 30, 80% also said buying or owning a home is harder for their generation than previous generations, while 71% saw worse prospects for financial security and 64% for having a good job. Among those under 30, 61% described the U.S. economic system as somewhat or very unfair. Socialism was viewed positively by 43%, compared with 38% for capitalism. By contrast, 30% of those 65 and older viewed socialism positively and 59% viewed capitalism positively.

I think the main reason Raspberry Pi succeeds is the quality of the software ecosystem. There are plenty of other SBCs out there, but most of them have very poor software support. The vendor typically cobbles together a janky Linux distro, throws it over the wall and says "OK, it's done!" and never provides upgrades or much in the way of assistance. Raspberry Pi hardware might be meh and it's certainly not as open as I like, but the software support is the best there is for SBCs. If other SBC vendors want to compete, they'll have to up their software game (and work with the community to get any modifications upstreamed into mainstream distros.) Absolutely, and by "meh", it's often "fine" or "adequate". And there's nothing wrong with adequate it is by definition good enough. Would I like a machine that's 3x as fast for half the price and fully open source? Well of course. Do I need one? Well... no. I don't think I've ever been in a situation were one of those would be necessary and a Pi would be inadequate. I've used NVidia Jetson Orin AGXs, but nothing is comparable to them anyway, even a 5x faster pi wouldn't match it. Yes, I agree. I run most of my life on a Pi 4: Internet router, Asterisk PBX, SMTP and IMAP server, file backup server, pi-hole, Radicale calendar/contact server, Xymon monitor, etc, etc, and it's just fine. It was pretty cheap when I bought it a few years ago. Nowadays, a mini PC might be cheaper, but it would likely consume more power, and low power consumption and silent operation was important to me for a server that's on 24x7. I guess it's a moot point if I can or cannot upgrade one if they're several hundred pounds and won't ship until three years from now if at all. People were buying these with the lowest ram, sourcing crappy questionable ram and upgrading them then reselling at the higher cost which left people eventually holding the bag when the memory errors and such appeared. When you get one and flash the official image the "lock" is in effect. Anyone can make their own image without it thus the end user tinkerers can STILL UPGRADE THEIR RAM. This just stops someone from selling a knock-off upgraded PI as "genuine" because people will likely expect to flash official images on it. This just thwarts those plans and keeps the higher quality chips around for people. If you don't like this buy the cheap one, get the ram chip and ugrade it and remove the lock yourself. Tinkering still works.... Unsoldering the ram chips and replacing them takes some effort and skill, and then you end up paying for 2 sets of ram chips - the old ones (which you cant do anything with) and the new ones. Given the effort and cost involved, this is only worth doing if: 1) the higher ram model is heavily marked up vs the incremental component cost so buying the ram yourself is significantly cheaper.2) the same ram is genuinely expensive, but much cheaper ram is available and still works without noticeable side effects Your comment is like pretending random knock-off USB flash memory that reports 256GB will actually hold the full amount. It's pretending the cheap knock-off sdcards on amazon don't noticeably lose data earlier or run slower. Pretending knock-off SSDs don't wrap around to the beginning earlier than their reported sizes. I don't want those people touching my hardware before me and not telling me. Cheaper or not. Real geeks boot with the front panel toggle switches. You still need a working Raspberry pi to do that :) https://obsolescence.wixsite.c... [wixsite.com] I have indeed now keyed a few basic programs in at boot time (well for the emulator not the pi obviously!). It's amazingly good fun and I then understood why octal rather than hex has such pride of place in unix and C. Ah, yes, the good old "this thing is completely unacceptable and evil except when our guys do it". Oh yeah? Try to buy a Pi Zero 2 W. You might find one, at no where near the $15 list price. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Kamala Harris Is Quietly Reaching Out to the Voters She Thought She Didn’t Need in 2024. They’re Listening—Carefully.
2026-09-25

Kamala Harris Is Quietly Reaching Out to the Voters She Thought She Didn’t Need in 2024. They’re Listening—Carefully.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. As the national political environment for Democrats shifts rapidly, Kamala Harris is teasing another run for president. At Al Sharpton’s National Action Network convention, when a crowd began chanting “Run again!,” Harris smiled. “I might. I am thinking about it,” she said. In recent months, apparently as an early step, Harris reportedly reached out to New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani and others on the Democratic left. She also called someone named James Zogby. Zogby had been a leading voice warning Democrats in 2024 that their support for funding Israel’s war in Gaza would cost them the election. When Axios reported that Harris had privately called Zogby and others to try to mend the rift, he wouldn’t comment at the time. But in a recent interview, he confirmed the call to me—and said he liked what he heard. “We had a long talk,” he said, describing the call as Harris wanting to “correct the historical record” about what happened in 2024 and where her support lies. “I felt very—I felt very good about it. I think she’s a good person.” I met Zogby—the head of the Arab American Institute and, at 80, a seasoned Arab activist—two years ago at the Democratic National Convention in Chicago. He was trying to convince a band of activists and around 30 delegates of the Uncommitted National Movement, representing some 700,000 voters opposed to the war, that the Democratic Party might actually be listening to them. For the first time in the party’s history, he noted then, the DNC had officially sanctioned a panel on Palestinian human rights. Dr. Tanya Haj-Hassan, a pediatric intensive care physician who had volunteered in Gaza, described the horrific scenes she witnessed. Hala Hijazi, a longtime Democratic organizer, talked about relatives she had lost. Layla Elabed, one of the co-founders of the “uncommitted movement,” sat on the panel alongside Zogby, who had been having versions of this fight inside the Democratic Party for almost four decades. “We’ve come a long way,” he told the crowd, explaining what it meant that the conversation was happening at all. “It is something I do not want to dismiss for a minute as to how significant this is, about what message the Harris campaign is sending by saying, ‘We want to talk about this and hear you talk about it.’ ” At the time, that did not sound entirely naive. Joe Biden had dropped out of the race less than a month earlier. Harris had taken over the campaign, and people in the uncommitted movement were cautiously hopeful that that might mean a change in policy. Harris and her campaign had recently spoken privately with uncommitted organizers Elabed and Abbas Alawieh in Michigan. And behind the scenes, conversations continued over a particularly modest request: Let a Palestinian American be one of the 236 speakers to address the convention from the main stage. They had spent months being told some version of the same thing: Donald Trump would be catastrophically worse for Palestinians, and therefore anything that endangered Harris was irresponsible. But they pressed ahead. “The people are with us on this,” Alawieh told me at the convention. Change became harder to see as the week went on. The conversations behind the scenes at the convention culminated in Harris’ final answer for their request: no. There would be no Palestinian speaker. Protests broke out. In her address to the convention, Harris did describe the suffering in Gaza as “devastating” and said Palestinians deserved “dignity, security, freedom, and self-determination.” That part received intense applause. It wasn’t enough. Harris lost Michigan to Trump by under 100,000 votes and lost the election. Trump returned to the White House. The activists I spoke to argued that Harris lost because she didn’t heed their warnings, though there was ultimately a lot that went wrong. Since 2024, Democratic voters have moved substantially further away from Israel. Candidates attacking unconditional support for the Israeli government have won Democratic primaries. More than 100 House Democrats recently voted to eliminate billions in military assistance to Israel. And several of the people who spent the 2024 convention trying to convince Democrats that they were attempting to save the party, not sabotage it, have spent the intervening years burrowing further into Democratic politics. Zogby told me he is still angry about what happened in 2024 but doesn’t squarely blame it on Harris’ position on Gaza. He mostly thinks the campaign failed by overrelying on political consultants and pollsters. He said he had reason to believe that Harris’ own instincts might have led somewhere different in that election but that she never had enough control over her own campaign to follow them. He had spoken with her earlier in the war, before she became the nominee, and told her to give a speech acknowledging what Palestinians were enduring and offering some recognition of their rights and their future. She told him she was already scheduled to speak that night. “Perfect. Do it. Work it in,” Zogby recalled telling her. When she told him the teleprompters were already prepared, he advised her to go off script. “Bill Clinton’s best lines were off script,” he said, laughing, as he recalled the conversation. The next day, he said, someone from her staff sent him a clip of Harris speaking about Palestinian rights. Gaza, Zogby predicts, will play an outsized role in the upcoming election. “It’ll be a litmus test for ‘Are you ready for primetime or not?’ ” he said. “And not just ready for primetime in terms of the Middle East, but are you willing to stand up for a principled approach to foreign policy? Are you going to continue to mouth the nonsense that has gotten us into this hole and has discredited the Democratic Party in the eyes of its base for not being able to take a principled position not just on Gaza, but on so many other issues? “We’re wimps,” Zogby said. “And one of the ways you show you’re not a wimp is Gaza, because that was the key way you became a wimp.” Leaders of the 2024 uncommitted movement are also watching these developments carefully. Elabed was among the Democratic activists organizing locally, then nationally. She and Alawieh briefly met Harris at a campaign stop near Detroit before the convention. Two years later, Elabed still remembers the encounter more favorably than someone hearing her criticisms of Harris might expect. “I always tell people, even to this day, when I spoke with her and I spoke about what my community was experiencing, what I personally was experiencing as a Palestinian American that had family in Palestine, her sympathy and empathy toward me and my community did feel really genuine,” she told me. “And I think that she was at that moment interested in talking more about the impact of U.S. funding to Israel on everyday people living here in Michigan and across the country, and what that meant for our families back home.” Harris later wrote in her memoir 107 Days that she told Biden that his public comments about innocent Gazans could come across as “inadequate and forced.” She also wrote that activists threatening to withhold their votes had felt “reckless” to her. I asked Elabed if she felt that line was directed at her. “I wish that her memoir was a little bit more telling,” she said. “I always wondered throughout her campaign why she would not deviate from Biden. Not just on the genocide, but, like, on any policy.” Elabed told me that if Harris runs again, “I would hope that she would be able to run genuinely and authentically as herself, by herself.” Headed into 2028, Congress is beginning to reflect the movement among Democratic voters. In July, Rep. Thomas Massie, a Kentucky Republican who opposes foreign aid, prompted a House vote on eliminating $3.3 billion in annual assistance to Israel. The amendment failed 314–104. But 103 of the 104 votes to cut the aid came from Democrats—nearly half the caucus. That would have been unthinkable a decade ago. The activist infrastructure has changed too. Alawieh, one of the people who spent the 2024 convention pressing Democrats for a Palestinian speaker, recently won the Democratic nomination for a Michigan state Senate seat. (Alawieh also got a call from Harris.) Abdul El-Sayed and other candidates running on staunch anti-war and economic-populist politics have won high-profile Democratic contests. The people who were standing outside the convention two years ago aren’t waiting outside anymore. In a further sign of the times, this week, Harris endorsed El-Sayed and was stumping for him in Michigan. Near the end of our conversation, I asked Zogby the question that hangs over Harris now. Given everything that happened, after what he heard on the call, did she really overcome 2024 and become the kind of candidate Democrats like him can support? “I think she could,” Zogby said. “I do. I do, I do. I think she deserves the chance to do it. And I hope she takes advantage of that chance and does it.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Latest Sign Donald Trump Is Feeling Scared About the Midterms
2026-09-24

The Latest Sign Donald Trump Is Feeling Scared About the Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. For weeks, the country has braced for Donald Trump’s next move to help his party win the midterm elections. Would the president find a workaround to restrict mail voting? Would he try to declare martial law? Trump’s latest gambit turns out to be both less inventive and similarly norm-breaking: a TV ad apparently paid for by U.S. taxpayers. The ad is part of a long history of this administration trying to use federal resources to boost GOP candidates in defiance of the long-standing, bipartisan view that governing and politicking should remain as separate as possible. Let’s dive in. It’s a lot. The ad (which you can watch here) opens with audio of Trump pledging to “defeat communism, socialism, and Marxism in America” and ends with Ultimate Fighting Championship CEO Dana White praising him as “the toughest, most resilient person that I’ve ever met.” In between, rapid-fire images of American flags, military aircraft, and the president looking tough flit across the screen, punctuated by red block letters of Trump victories (“LARGEST TAX CUTS IN HISTORY,” “REIGNITING AMERICAN MANUFACTURING,” etc.). The whole 30-second shebang is set to the JMSN song “Love Me.” As unsubtle as all of that is, the real doozy drops in the final seconds, when a disclaimer appears stating that the commercial was “Paid for by the U.S. Government.” It has reportedly aired on Newsmax and Fox News so far. In a statement to the New York Times on Thursday, the White House called the ad an “educational and unapologetically patriotic” public service announcement. Uh-huh, sure. And this isn’t the first time this administration has blurred the line between governing and campaigning, right? No sir. As Republicans confront the possibility of a calamitous midterm, several Trump Cabinet secretaries have hit the campaign trail. Bucking the post-Watergate norm that the nation’s top law-enforcement official is supposed to avoid the appearance of partisan bias, Attorney General Todd Blanche has appeared on stage at events for Republican candidates in North Carolina and New York. Last month, Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth flew to Iowa on a taxpayer-funded military aircraft to attend a fundraiser for Rep. Zach Nunn, an endangered Republican. “We need guys like this man right here having our back in Congress,” Hegseth said. Other high-ranking Trump officials—including Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent—spoke at Trump’s midterm rally this month. Last week, Trump posted videos of himself endorsing a series of Republican candidates from the Oval Office itself. Surely there are laws to prevent this kind of thing? Enter the Hatch Act. Passed in 1939, the law bars federal employees from tapping official resources for political acts. It’s true that the president and vice president are exempt. But as the Washington Post explains, Congress has for decades included language in government funding bills banning the use of federal funds for “publicity or propaganda purposes,” including boosting political candidates or parties. Sending Cabinet officials to rallies, filming endorsements from the White House, and spending public money on a Trump-touting TV ad could violate both provisions. “This may be felony criminal theft and conversion of government property for political campaign purposes,” Rep. Jamie Raskin, a Maryland Democrat, said today of the ad, which he called a Hatch Act violation. OK, but couldn’t one argue that everything the president and his top officials do is political in some way? It’s a fair question, and past administrations of both parties have certainly found ways to duck the spirit of the law. When a president running for reelection visits a swing state to announce a new government initiative, are the federal employees who help write his speech involved in governing or campaign work? The Hatch Act also has some loopholes. The Pentagon says Hegseth visited Iowa in his “personal capacity” and that traveling aboard a military aircraft is a security necessity. The Justice Department says Blanche’s campaign “appearances are reviewed and cleared by the Department’s ethics officials” and that he reimburses travel expenses “in accordance with federal law, regulations, and policies.” So does the Hatch Act just not have any teeth? Historically, violations have rarely been punished. Government watchdogs concluded that Secretary of State Mike Pompeo, chief of staff Mark Meadows, and other top officials ran afoul of the Hatch Act during Trump’s first term. Under Joe Biden, press secretary Karine Jean-Pierre and Energy Secretary Jennifer Granholm faced similar accusations. None suffered meaningful consequences. Trump and his entourage evidently believe that a Republican-controlled Congress will never punish them, so they don’t have to comply with the spirit of any of these rules.Stretching the law is one thing; acting like it doesn’t exist is an escalation driven by a (perhaps justified) sense of impunity. Even if Democrats win back Congress this fall, it’s unlikely to be by a margin large enough to impeach, convict, and remove the president or his top officials. Still, Trump could pay in another way. Significant majorities of Americans already think government corruption has become endemic and many seem to blame Trump for it. If voters decide they don’t like Cabinet officials who treat their jobs like extensions of a political movement and presidents who treat the White House like a campaign backdrop, Trump’s efforts to rescue his party may fail. And if they do, future presidents tempted to follow his example may conclude that bending the rules just isn’t worth it. OK, let’s shift gears from midterm mischief to evening entertainment. My colleagues recommend that you: Appreciate a music legend’s legacy: Dolly Parton got her start in Nashville as a country star, but she cemented her status as a global icon by courting pop fans. On a new episode of Hit Parade, host Chris Molanphy explains how Dolly blazed a trail for today’s country/pop crossover musicians like “Choosin’ Texas” singer Ella Langley. Savor a celebrity rumor that was too good to be true: For a moment this week, it seemed like Channing Tatum was using a burner Instagram account to leave snarky comments on his ex’s posts. But—spoiler alert—the account isn’t his. Heather Schwedel breaks down this titillating gossip. Search for a song’s deeper meaning: Taylor Swift is expected to drop her new single, “Patient Zero,” early Friday morning, so let’s count down the hours with some speculation. Anna Gibbs suggests that the song could be a riff on the loaded history of this term. Contemplate some steamy movie scenes: Prime Video’s new movie The Love Hypothesis is an academic romance based on Star Wars fan fiction. Slate’s Rebecca Onion and Nadira Goffe tackle the most important question: How hot are the sex scenes? With that, enjoy the rest of your night! We’ll be back in your inbox tomorrow. What Are Progressives Supposed to Do About Public Education?
2026-09-24

What Are Progressives Supposed to Do About Public Education?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Over the weekend, the New York Times Magazine published a piece by journalist Nikole Hannah-Jones, in which she grapples with the decision to send her daughter to a low-income majority-Black poorly rated public school in her Brooklyn, New York, neighborhood. Hannah-Jones has made her career writing about race, class, and school segregation, and a decade ago she wrote about the choice to forgo the quote-unquote better school options those in her educational and economic cohort generally chose. “Saying my child deserved access to ‘good’ public schools felt like implying that children in ‘bad’ schools deserved the schools they got, too,” she wrote back in 2016. “I understood that so much of school segregation is structural—a result of decades of housing discrimination, of political calculations and the machinations of policy makers, of simple inertia. But I also believed that it is the choices of individual parents that uphold the system, and I was determined not to do what I’d seen so many others do when their values about integration collided with the reality of where to send their own children to school.” Ten years later, her daughter is 16, and old enough to have a say. And for high school, she made about as different a choice as possible, opting into one that is highly rated and selective—and majority white and Asian. And as she’s realized just how behind she is at her new school, and all she hadn’t learned at her previous ones, she flat-out told her mom that she feels betrayed. For elementary school and middle school, Hannah-Jones’ daughter, Najya, went to schools where the lunchroom was a chaotic scrum, hallway fights happened often, teachers spent an enormous amount of time on disruptive students to the neglect of those who were there to learn, homework wasn’t assigned, in-class assignments weren’t corrected, and even though she got A’s in math, it turns out she couldn’t do basic algebra or even long division by high school. When she made the decision to put her daughter in a troubled school, Hannah-Jones wrote that “I also knew that we would be able to make up for Najya anything the school was lacking.” That turned out not to be the case. Intensive math tutoring seems to have gotten her up to speed. But that came after failing a state math exam, something Hannah-Jones wrote off as her daughter simply struggling with standardized tests. And it came with tanking confidence and growing anxiety from a kid who is clearly bright, curious, and highly capable. The response to Hannah-Jones’ update has been overwhelming. Many people (myself included) applauded her for her honesty and her ability to look quite critically at how her own choices—made in accordance with strongly held and admirable principles—may have harmed the one person in the world she’s supposed to most ferociously protect. Others picked apart her parenting choices, mocked the very principles that informed her decisions, or turned the problems in some public-school classrooms into ones caused by their own pet theories. There were grosser reactions, which I suspect stem from some conservatives’ continued obsessions with battling another Hannah-Jones endeavor, the 1619 Project, that are well worth ignoring entirely. At its core, though, Hannah-Jones’ article is as much about the shameful realities of too many American public schools (especially those that serve Black and brown students) as it is much more fundamental questions: What is the purpose of public education? What do we owe everyone’s children? And what specific obligations do we owe to our own? These questions were political fault lines well before this story published. To many conservatives, this seems obvious: You have obligations only to your own child, what you owe anyone else’s is minimal, and your own authority over your children is ultimate and nearly unlimited. The state, in this view, shouldn’t have much of a role in educating children in the first place—that is the job of the parent (never mind that more than a quarter of American adults are nearly or functionally illiterate and 15 percent cannot do the most basic math, such as adding or subtracting). When it comes to what you owe your child, well, that should also be up to the parent. Pro-family conservatives routinely oppose basic child protection laws, like having social workers check up on homeschooling families or bar people convicted of crimes against children from homeschooling. They have fought efforts to ban child marriage and to criminalize the practice of teachers hitting or otherwise abusing children in the classroom (including disabled students). Empowered within the Trump administration, they have set about dismantling the Department of Education and America’s long, proud, and astoundingly economically fruitful tradition of publicly educating all of its residents. Progressives and liberals see it differently, to put things mildly. Foundational to both worldviews is the idea that citizens should have equal rights and opportunities. Public education serves that purpose: Without it, individual opportunity waxes to extremes and wanes down to near-nothing according to the incomes of one’s parents. Public education is also broadly socially beneficial: The more educated the populace, the better able it is to participate in our democratic processes and to contribute to the economy. The abilities to logically reason, to analyze, and to work through complex problems are skills built by literacy that also contribute to a more productive and peaceful polity. When kids from less-fortunate families have access to a good education, they have the chance to pull themselves up and bring subsequent generations along with them. These ideals, though, have crashed into the hard reality of American life and our country’s history. Equal opportunity has never really existed, especially for Black people, in America. The truth of our history is that an entire population was brought here and forced into slavery, their families separated, their rights zero. They were barred from learning how to read, let alone from getting to vote or even being considered full citizens. That was followed by decades of legally enforced segregation and discrimination and policies that are hard to see as anything but either intentionally or negligently impoverishing. While the era of legal segregation in schools and elsewhere has ended, segregation itself has not. American public schools, including those in minority-white New York City, remain sharply segregated along racial and economic lines. The question of how to fix this is one that has roiled the broader left for decades, and that animated Hannah-Jones’ initial decisions about her daughter’s education. In one telling, Hannah-Jones seemed to believe that her obligation to everyone’s children and to the public school system generally was more important than any obligation to her own child. There are many progressives who make this argument: One idea is more or less that no child is a special snowflake with more rights than any other, and so in the interests of equity, you should curtail your privileged child’s opportunities. (“They’ll be fine” is a typical refrain here.) Another, more compelling argument is that if enough privileged parents send their kids to “bad” schools, then eventually the “bad” schools will improve, and that these are basic social obligations we owe one another. (A less-convincing corollary is that the “bad” schools aren’t bad at all, it’s just racist parents who believe that some schools are bad—an argument starkly contradicted by Hannah-Jones’ experience, as well as things like test scores, which, to be fair, many detractors also say are racially biased.) The problem, though, is that “Sacrifice your child’s education because they are not special and other kids need more” is not exactly a compelling sell to many parents. This is a broader problem with many progressive efforts: Without the ability to immediately overhaul all of society, making outcomes more equal turns instead into a race to the bottom. But everyone being equally poorly off isn’t particularly appealing to most people. It also runs counter to most parents’ impulses, which are to protect their children and to expand their universes of opportunity. This is of course not true for all parents, which is also an inconvenient truth that both conservatives and progressives seem to sidestep. Some parents are truly awful: abusive, neglectful, don’t care if their child gets a decent education or becomes a violent hellion who makes life for his teachers and classmates hell. Many more are simply incapable of good parenting, so caught up in their own struggles they cannot give their children even the basics. Many conservatives are eager to heap scorn on and blame these parents, but then their answer for what to do to help their children is a shrug—or, worse: expel or jail the most troubled kids, which can wreak generational havoc. Too many progressives, on the other hand, refuse to acknowledge that many of the problems plaguing public schools do in fact stem from dysfunctional family situations and checked-out educators, and that that reality—not just bigotry—might shape the willingness of more-engaged families to send their children to institutions where dysfunction is the norm and learning is limited. In truth, most parents will put their own child’s needs first, even if they say they don’t believe their child should be treated better than any other. You buy your kid a coat; you may donate one to a homeless shelter, but you probably aren’t buying coats for every kid in America (or every kid the world over). This is a good thing. Efforts to sever a parent’s particular and deep ties to their own offspring run counter to the basic biological imperatives that enable the continuity of the human race, and routinely end in tragedy. But it’s also intolerable to live in a society that forgoes its obligations to other people in it—especially children, who are any society’s smallest, most malleable, and most vulnerable members, and those who will shape its future. These two things—obligations to one’s own child, obligations to everyone’s children—can sometimes be in tension. But they don’t have to be in total conflict if we adopt a general politics of understanding that the things you want for your child are probably similar to the things other children need, too—and if you can see advocating for your child and advocating for the needs of others as mutually reinforcing rather than a zero-sum game. Education is a good example. American public schools were not bestowed upon us by God; we can change how we fund them, how we staff them, and what we expect from them. We can also take a view that I suspect is held by Hannah-Jones, that a good education means more than algebra and literacy (although it obviously has to mean those things, too). A good education also means experiencing the friction and the sparks of interacting with people who are different from you—whose families look different from your own, whose assumptions and priors do not match yours, who have more resources than you, who have less. A good education also does start at home—and that means raising your children to know that you, the parent, have their back and are their ultimate and unwavering advocate, and also that you’re modeling how to be a decent person in the world, which means caring about other people, too. What does this mean in the real world? It won’t be the same for every family. At the very least, I would argue that it means supporting evidence-based and appropriately funded public education that rejects the dogmas and ideologies du jour in favor of rigorous academics, high expectations, and ample support to help students achieve what they can. It means not lowering the bar just because some students have a harder time clearing it, but figuring out how to give those students the help they need to get over it. One mistake progressives have made in the past few years is seeing differences in outcome as evidence of a fatal flaw in whatever is measuring those outcomes (standardized tests, for example), rather than as weather vanes pointing toward what needs more attention. The removal of test scores in college admissions and the growing hostility toward putting high-achieving kids into gifted programs feed into this sense that progressive education policy is more about taking away than building up—that the promise of equality isn’t about lifting up the less fortunate, but tearing down those who are doing well. Appropriately balancing your obligations often means giving particular consideration to your own child, who is, after all, here only because you chose to bring them into the world. Making schools and society better does not have to start with making things for your child worse. It does mean, though, figuring out what “better” means. Are rankings and test scores the be-all and end-all to what you would consider a good education? Or do you care as much about your child being curious even if there’s no professional or rational economic purpose behind it? How much value do you put behind your child learning how to navigate a diversity of experiences and interactions with many different kinds of people? With the right political choices, we can have all of the above. But we’re not going to get there if people on the left start from a place of presumed scarcity, demanding that individual parents make intolerable sacrifices that set back the people they love most, and if the progressive message is that the only way to even the scales is by setting both sides to zero. www.theregister.com - Articles
Office 2016 and 2019 holdouts blame update for license deactivation bug
2026-09-25

Office 2016 and 2019 holdouts blame update for license deactivation bug

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
But was the update at fault? Microsoft isn't saying Users report that a recent optional update for out-of-date Microsoft 365 applications might be connected to suddenly deactivated Microsoft Office 2016 and 2019 installations. The activation bug cropped up this week and appears to mostly impact Home and Business perpetual license installations, stripping away their authenticated status and forcing users into a loop where they are asked over and over again to re-enter their product key or face deactivation or reduced-functionality mode. The symptom is that a previously activated copy of Office 2016 and 2019 appears to abruptly lose its activation information, triggering alarming pop-ups and dire warnings that functionality loss is imminent. Affected users say one fix is to re-enter the original product key to restore the activation state. This would be a fine solution, if the user is sufficiently organized and have that number or numbers to hand. It is not so fine if you're faced with dozens, hundreds, or even more devices affected. A Register reader told us the issue "took out our Office 2016 installations." They added: "First indication is a message in the app you are using telling you that normal functionality has been removed. Also Yellow banners saying you have five days left before you can no longer use the apps. "It takes multiple product reactivations, restarts and sign in/sign out of your Office account to get it stable." A glance online shows many users believe the problem is connected to KB5002907. The optional update is for Windows PCs with Microsoft 365 Apps that are more than 90 days out of date. According to Microsoft, "Installing the update can help the apps resume updating and move to a current build for the device’s assigned update channel." Crucially, Microsoft also said the update is not automatic. However, users affected by the sudden reactivation requirement noted that the issue began around the same time the update appeared. Support ended for Office 2016 and 2019 on October 14, 2025. As such, Microsoft is under no obligation to release updates for the software. That, however, hasn't stopped it from occasionally releasing a patch that breaks stuff, for example the Paste problems that surfaced after an Excel patch. Unexpected consequences from an Microsoft 365 update are not unusual. Microsoft also has form when it comes to bricking old versions of software, although in the case of the Apple version of Office 2019, that bricking was deliberate due to the expiration of a certificate that dropped the applications into "reduced functionality mode" – opening and printing still worked, but creating, editing or saving? Perhaps sir or madam might consider a subscription to Microsoft 365? As a reminder, when Office 2016 and 2019 for Windows reached the end of support, Microsoft assured users they could continue using the products regardless. It did not mention the unexpected need to enter a product activation code… ® But was the update at fault? Which copy of that file is the real one? Dinner, off the record, in Midtown
2026-09-25

Which copy of that file is the real one? Dinner, off the record, in Midtown

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Joe Fay chairs a Register dinner in New York on the file infrastructure nobody has got around to replacing, with nothing on the record. EVENT: The Register is hosting a private dinner in New York on Tuesday 27 October for senior technology, infrastructure and data leaders, with LucidLink. Joe Fay takes the chair, it runs under the Chatham House Rule, and nothing said leaves the table. Distributed teams are producing more unstructured file data than ever, and most of them reach it through infrastructure designed for an office: legacy NAS, a VPN, and a sync-and-download habit nobody actually chose. It rarely fails outright. It charges rent somewhere else instead, in duplicated storage, in waiting, in governance gaps, and in the hour somebody loses working out which version of a file is current. The conversation worth having about that is not the architecture diagram. It is what a team did when it stopped being in one building. Which workaround somebody has quietly been paying for ever since. What broke when they tried to retire the NAS, and what they would do differently now. People say that over dinner. They do not say it on a recorded webinar. So this is dinner. Drinks, a handful of questions across the table, then more drinks. Everyone there as a peer, and a chair whose job includes stopping anyone who starts to pitch. The premise is deliberately not rip and replace. The evening is about modernizing around the storage and governance investments an organization already has, rather than pretending they are not there, which is usually the point at which these conversations stop being useful to the people who have to pay for them. On the table: handling the growth in unstructured data, supporting distributed teams without paying for the same file three times, sync and version control, and whether any of it holds up against a security incident. Holly Bossert, Commercial Sales US at LucidLink, joins the conversation. Joe Fay of The Register moderates. The table is for senior technology, infrastructure and data leaders carrying this now, not planning for it later. Dinner runs from 6.00pm to 8.30pm at The Capital Grille, 120 W 51st Street. Places are limited and go by invitation. But was the update at fault? Raspberry Pi surfs memory crunch to record first half
2026-09-25

Raspberry Pi surfs memory crunch to record first half

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
A well-timed RAM stockpile helped keep boards shipping while rivals struggled for memory Raspberry Pi has posted a record first half after a well-timed memory stockpile helped it ride out soaring prices and shortages. The Cambridge-based company reported revenue of $256.9 million for the six months ended June 30, up 90 percent from $135.5 million a year earlier, while pre-tax profit more than tripled to $19.6 million. Raspberry Pi shipped 4.2 million boards during the six-month period, up 17 percent year-on-year, while its order backlog doubled to 2.6 million units. Behind some of those rather healthy numbers is a bet Raspberry Pi made last year, when it built up a sizeable stash of memory before the market tightened. "The decision in FY 2025 to build significant strategic memory inventory has allowed us to maintain product availability at a time when smaller competitors have struggled to secure allocation," CEO Eben Upton said. It also meant Raspberry Pi spent much of the first half burning through memory bought at considerably friendlier prices. Gross profit per board climbed from $8 a year ago to $12.20, helped by price rises on R Pi's products as well as cheaper memory inventory acquired during 2025. Overall gross profit rose 79 percent to $59.4 million. That particular party is winding down. Raspberry Pi said the "exceptional unit economics" seen in the first half have since moderated as the cheaper memory bought during 2025 has been used up. It hasn't stopped stockpiling – it's just paying a lot more to do it. The average cost of memory in its inventory stood at $13.30 per GB at the end of June, up from $3.60 per GB at the end of 2025. At the end of June, Raspberry Pi was holding 2.4 million LPDDR4 devices representing 5.8 million GB of memory. By the end of August, that had grown to 8.3 million GB – around three months of sales for the products that use it. Between that stockpile and confirmed orders, Raspberry Pi said it has enough memory to meet its production targets for the rest of 2026, with further purchases planned to ensure it enters 2027 with significant inventory. However, the shortage hasn't left it entirely unscathed. Pi Zero sales fell 9 percent after production congestion at a Taiwanese company packaging one of its core components restricted supply. Demand hasn't disappeared: Raspberry Pi has about 1 million Pi Zero units sitting on back order. Elsewhere, customers appear willing to swallow the higher prices forced by rising memory costs. Direct sales of Raspberry Pi 4 and Pi 5 boards increased 69 percent and 47 percent respectively, with the company reporting continued demand for the pricier 8GB variants. OEM customers are also becoming an increasingly important part of the business. Direct shipments rose 26 percent to 3.4 million units, with Raspberry Pi pointing to particularly strong demand from the smart home and aerospace and defense sectors. The company expects to ship more units in the second half than the first, despite the ongoing squeeze on memory supplies. For Raspberry Pi, planning for the great memory squeeze has so far been rather more lucrative than painful. Buying the RAM before everyone desperately needed it turned out to be a useful trick. ® But was the update at fault? Windows update leaves some virtual desktops staring into the void
2026-09-25

Windows update leaves some virtual desktops staring into the void

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Users may need to summon Explorer manually after signing in Microsoft has admitted that some Windows 11 virtual desktops may fail to load after sign-in, leaving users staring at a black screen until they launch Explorer manually. The glitch affects devices running the August 2026 non-security preview update or later releases and, according to Microsoft, "has been primarily observed on Azure Virtual Desktop (AVD) hosts, using FSLogix." Windows Explorer crashes may appear in the affected device's Application event log. Users can temporarily recover the desktop by opening Task Manager and running explorer.exe as a new task. Microsoft has also issued a Known Issue Rollback (KIR) that administrators can deploy through Group Policy. This disables the offending change until a permanent fix arrives. "We are working on a resolution for this issue, and it will be released in a future Windows update," the company said. This is distinct from another problem introduced by the same August preview update, which replaced some customized desktop backgrounds with solid black. Microsoft fixed that in September, but has not explained precisely what causes this latest failure. It said only that the problem "appears to occur more frequently with some existing user profiles." That will be of limited comfort to users glumly firing up Task Manager to launch their desktops manually. Windows 11 versions 26H1, 25H2, and 24H2 are affected. Microsoft does not list Windows 10 or Windows Server among the affected platforms. The stream of problems introduced by Microsoft's August and September updates is becoming relentless, undermining the company's claims that Windows is getting better. Quality improvement promises made earlier this year by Windows chief Pavan Davuluri are ringing increasingly hollow. If this is how Microsoft plans to "win back" its fans, as Satya Nadella said it must, the response may well be: thanks, but no thanks. ® But was the update at fault? Uncle Sam wants in on Musk's €120M fight with Brussels
2026-09-25

Uncle Sam wants in on Musk's €120M fight with Brussels

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Justice Department claims the EU overreached by pursuing X's owner and wider corporate empire The US government wants to join Elon Musk and X's legal challenge to the European Commission's €120 million fine against the social media platform. The Commission issued its first fine under the Digital Services Act (DSA) in December 2025, finding that X had breached transparency rules through its paid blue checkmarks, advertising repository, and restrictions on researchers' access to public data. X owner Elon Musk described the decision as "crazy" and "insane" in a Xeet, and compared the "woke" Commission to "Stasi commissars," combining two rather different authoritarian references. Before issuing a response, he shared the comments made by FCC chair Brendan Carr, who accused "Europe" of "fining a successful US tech company for being a successful US tech company." "Europe is taxing Americans to subsidize a continent held back by Europe's own suffocating regulations," Carr added. US politicians also rallied around Musk. Senator Ted Cruz called the fine "an abomination" and an attack on American free speech. The US government has now applied to intervene in the cases brought by Musk and X Internet and X Holdings, supporting their attempts to annul the Commission's decision. Assistant Attorney General Brett A. Shumate accused the Commission of regulatory overreach against US companies beyond its jurisdiction. "The European Commission inappropriately attempted to expand its regulatory authority to reach American companies not present or operating within its jurisdiction," he said. "We will not tolerate the European Commission engaging in regulatory overreach to try and control American engines of innovation and economic growth." The Justice Department said it has "significant concerns" about how the Commission determined which people and companies constituted the provider of X's digital service. It intends to argue that the decision conflicts with accepted limits on extraterritorial jurisdiction and the principle that companies are ordinarily legally distinct from their owners and shareholders. It was especially concerned that the Commission's approach "extended legal scrutiny to Mr Musk himself as a private individual" and implicated other US companies under his control. According to the DoJ, the Commission found the parties jointly and severally liable for the €120 million fine and calculated it using the worldwide turnover of the corporate group ultimately controlled by Musk. The US government argues that allowing this approach to stand could have broader consequences for American-owned digital platforms operating in the EU. The DoJ stated: "If the approach in the European Commission Decision is upheld, this may have significant implications for 'very large online platforms' and other similar entities operating in the digital services sector within the EU, many of which are domiciled (at parent level) within the United States. "The United States looks forward to the opportunity to address these concerns and issues in detail before the General Court." Musk bought Twitter in 2022 and subsequently replaced its identity-based verification system with a subscription offering. Blue checkmarks, previously awarded by Twitter to accounts it considered authentic and notable, became available to paying users without equivalent identity checks. The change swiftly produced impersonation and spam, and the Commission concluded that presenting those accounts as "verified" amounted to deceptive design. The Commission also found that X's advertising repository lacked required information and imposed access barriers that prevented effective scrutiny of advertisements. Separately, it said X placed unnecessary obstacles in the way of researchers seeking public platform data. The violations behind the EU fine are separate from matters under investigation in the UK, including X's handling of illegal hate speech and terrorist content flowing through its feeds and Grok's highly controversial nudifying functionality. The Commission has since accepted X's plan to address the advertising repository and researcher access violations. The €120 million penalty and the legal theory used to calculate and impose it remain the subjects of the challenges now before the General Court – and, if its application succeeds, of an intervention by the US government. ® But was the update at fault? Asahi fork embraces LLMs and lands Linux on the M4 Mac mini
2026-09-25

Asahi fork embraces LLMs and lands Linux on the M4 Mac mini

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Coding agents helped two developers build an accelerated desktop and graphics driver in weeks Gravity Linux is a newly announced distro targeting newer Apple Silicon Macs, and its first experimental alpha already boots an accelerated desktop on the M4 Mac mini. The project homepage says: "Gravity Linux is a Fedora remix that brings a fast, modern Linux desktop to Apple Silicon – built in the open, by people like you." If this sounds familiar, the About page explains: "Gravity Linux was initially forked from Asahi Linux over differences in policy, namely regarding LLM use. We have tremendous respect for the Asahi Linux project and emphasize our shared goal of running Linux on Apple Silicon." There's already an early alpha release for the M4 Mac mini, a model launched in October 2024 just days after the company announced the M4 iMac. It includes GPU acceleration with OpenGL ES 3.0 and OpenGL 3.3 support, but is explicitly aimed at developers rather than daily use. HDMI output works, but USB-C displays, USB4, Thunderbolt, and suspend do not, while power management remains incomplete. In terms of hardware generations, this puts Gravity ahead of Asahi Linux. As The Register reported earlier this month, Asahi has only recently added preliminary M3 support, for now without graphics acceleration. The comparison is not exact, however: Gravity's M4 release is an early developer alpha, while Asahi is a longer-established project supporting a much broader range of hardware. Apple, meanwhile, has already moved on: The Reg reported on the first M6-based Macs last month, nearly three years after the company launched its "scary fast" M3 systems. The Reg reported on the new Asahi Linux project in March 2022, and tried it out later the same month. However, Asahi has since lost several prominent contributors. Project lead Hector Martin resigned in February 2025. A week later, the leader of the GPU effort, known only as "Asahi Lina," also quit. As we mentioned later that year, in August 2025, the leader of the GPU driver effort, Alyssa Rosenzweig, left the project. She had already, in her own words, passed the reins on to her other Arm GPU drivers that April. As her resume [PDF] shows, she's been working for Intel since then. Gravity attributes much of its rapid progress to a development method that Asahi explicitly prohibits. Project lead Cody Ho previously worked at both Apple and OpenAI, and has published a two-part account of using coding agents for Apple Silicon reverse engineering. On August 3, Ho published the first part of "I Came, I Prompted, I Left," describing the construction of a custom hypervisor for newer Apple Silicon. The second part, published on September 15, explains how he and co-developer Niklas Sheth built an M4 GPU driver in about a month. Ho says much of the work was performed through largely unattended LLM loops. This is one of the sharpest differences between Gravity Linux and Asahi Linux. Ho and Sheth are reverse-engineering Apple's hardware with the assistance of AI tools, while the Asahi project has a Generative AI (LLM) Policy that says "we broadly forbid the use of generative AI tooling for material contributions to Asahi Linux." Gravity's LLM Use and Clean-Room Policy permits such tools but attempts to contain the legal risks through separation. It states: "If in any session your LLM has looked at any disassembly, decompilation, or other protected implementation details of an Apple component, you must consider yourself tainted with respect to that component." This does not mean nobody involved looks at Apple binaries. Ho says that while developing the hypervisor, he used an LLM to disassemble Apple's proprietary SPTM component. That made him ineligible to write the corresponding clean-room implementation, so the knowledge had to be documented and handed to someone who had not seen the protected material. Ho and Sheth say the GPU work followed a stricter route, using hardware traces, live probing and self-written shaders without inspecting Apple binaries. They also acknowledge benefiting from Asahi's earlier M1 and M2 work and using its existing userspace API. The result is nevertheless striking: Gravity's first alpha brings an accelerated Linux desktop to an M4 Mac mini, and Ho credits LLMs with helping the pair achieve in weeks work that would ordinarily take far longer. But the code still requires extensive testing, human review, and refactoring before it can be upstreamed. Gravity's developers warn that its kernel driver may have to wait for Asahi's M1 and M2 driver to land upstream first. Gravity has therefore produced an intriguing demonstration of what AI-assisted reverse engineering can do, rather than a controlled experiment proving that Asahi's policy is what held it back. ® But was the update at fault? Microsoft isn't saying Joe Fay chairs a Register dinner in New York on the file infrastructure nobody has got around to replacing, with nothing on the record. Cricut’s new compact crafter prints, cuts, and laminates stickers
2026-09-25

Cricut’s new compact crafter prints, cuts, and laminates stickers

The Verge | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
﻿The new Cricut StickerPix machines might be the cheapest way to launch your Etsy shop. ﻿The new Cricut StickerPix machines might be the cheapest way to launch your Etsy shop. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Cricut announced its first crafting machines with printing capabilities that are primarily designed as all-in-one solutions for turning photos and other images into precut stickers. The Cricut StickerPix Print plus Cut and Cricut StickerPix Print are also two of the smallest machines in the company’s current lineup and seem ideal for those whose crafting aspirations may be limited by their available storage space. Starting at $299, the Cricut StickerPix Print plus Cut is the larger and more expensive of the two new machines and is also available as part of pricier bundles that include more consumables. It’s compatible with 4x7-inch sticker sheets and 4x6-inch photo paper and uses a dye-sublimation printing process that relies on heat instead of spraying ink for better color reproduction. Unlike Cricut’s larger crafting machines, these new offerings include a built-in lamination process making prints resistant to water, scratches, and fading over time. The smaller Cricut StickerPix Print starts at $169 and is also available as part of larger bundles. It carries forward most of the functionality of the larger model but without cutting capabilities. It instead prints directly to precut sticker sheets with shapes that include circles, rectangles, and squares, but it can also be used to print 4x6 photos. The biggest difference between the StickerPix Print plus Cut and Cricut’s larger machines is that it’s only capable of cutting through the top layer of a print, producing what are known as kiss-cut stickers that come out still attached to a larger sheet. Cricut’s other machines are capable of making die-cut stickers that are sliced right through the backing paper. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Gaming’s biggest horror series are more vital than ever
2026-09-25

Gaming’s biggest horror series are more vital than ever

The Verge | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Resident Evil and Silent Hill are both thriving with new spins on survival horror. Resident Evil and Silent Hill are both thriving with new spins on survival horror. For the third year in a row, I’ve been scared out of my wits by a new Silent Hill game. First it was the modern remake of Silent Hill 2, then the Japan-set spinoff Silent Hill F. Now I find myself slowly crawling through a quaint Scottish town in Silent Hill: Townfall, trying my best not to get brutally murdered by demonic creatures chasing me through the streets. It’s been a remarkable turnaround for a franchise that once seemed like it was on life support. And when you combine it with a strong run from Resident Evil across games and film, you have gaming’s two biggest horror franchises both at the height of their powers. For Resident Evil, it’s been a steady buildup, but it really started with Resident Evil 7 nearly a decade ago. It jolted the series back to life with a first-person perspective that made its scares feel more visceral. Since then, Capcom has settled on an effective strategy of alternating between bottom-up remakes of classics like Resident Evil 4, and new spins on the formula, like the vampire-tinged Village. This has allowed the studio to be more experimental with the mainline titles; this year’s Requiem, for instance, was a bold attempt to merge two types of Resident Evil games together, mixing straight-up action with first-person terror. Resident Evil has always had an uneven track record when it comes to adaptations, like the largely forgettable live-action Netflix series. But that may be changing. Last week a reboot of the film series premiered, called simply Resident Evil, helmed by Weapons director Zach Cregger. It explored the zombie franchise from a new viewpoint: Instead of following iconic characters like Leon or Claire, it was centered on a hapless delivery driver who served as a stand-in for a player who is terrible at survival horror games. Like the Exit 8 movie, Resident Evil explores its source material through the lens of the player. The result was equal parts hilarious and horrifying — there were scenes where gore dripped from ceilings, and a particularly terrifying long-limbed baby — and it also had the best box office opening in franchise history. Silent Hill has followed a similar trajectory, albeit on a smaller scale. After lying largely dormant following the 2015 cancellation of Silent Hills, a reboot led by Hideo Kojima and Guillermo del Toro, Konami signaled a return for the franchise with a series of announcements in 2022. Not everything has been a winner — a film came out earlier this year with little fanfare, and the less said about the bizarre interactive TV series Ascension the better — but when it comes to games Silent Hill has been on a roll. The Silent Hill 2 remake set the stage, introducing arguably the most beloved title in the series to a new audience, while Silent Hill F offered a freshly horrifying experience through its setting and more RPG-focused gameplay. Townfall likewise expands the idea of what a Silent Hill game can be. It launches this week on the PS5 and PC, and is developed by Screen Burn, an indie studio known for games like Observation. You play as a man named Simon who arrives at a small Scottish town where it’s immediately evident something is wrong; it seems completely deserted and is shrouded in the classic Silent Hill fog. Oh yeah, and screeching creatures ripped out of Hellraiser stalk the streets. From there you have to figure out what actually happened, and try your best not to die at the hands of a mutated monstrosity. What’s most notable about Townfall is how it plays. It has much in common with “walking simulator” games like Firewatch and Everybody’s Gone to the Rapture, where the experience is about exploring a space and uncovering a story organically. Here that means poking around in abandoned homes and medical centers, reading through files and listening to phone messages that reveal the extent of the town’s horrific history. The game then layers on more traditional horror elements. There’s some light combat, but mostly there’s a big focus on stealth: to get where you need to go you’ll often have to sneak past unpredictable monsters, who hunt mindlessly while screeching and writhing in pain. This plays out a little like Alien: Isolation; you have a handheld device that tells you where enemies are, and you can hide in closets and sheds in hopes they don’t spot you. It’s incredibly scary, and still feels intrinsically like a Silent Hill game, despite the shifts in gameplay and setting. The triumphant return of Resident Evil and Silent Hill is coming at a particularly exciting time for horror, with breakout successes at the box office and a promising wave of streaming series. The genre is having a moment — and despite being around for decades, both series are hitting a fresh stride at just the right time A free daily digest of the news that matters most.
