Can Apple Home’s AI camera features outsmart Amazon’s and Google’s? I put them to the test
2026-09-25

Can Apple Home’s AI camera features outsmart Amazon’s and Google’s? I put them to the test

The Verge | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Apple Intelligence has arrived on HomeKit Secure Video. Here’s how its new features compare with Ring and Nest — and how well each tells you what’s happening and what really matters. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. A few years back, I was at a beachside Easter egg hunt, watching my kids dash through sand dunes searching for sweet treats. My phone buzzed in my pocket; I ignored it. A moment later, it buzzed again. I pulled it out, glanced down, and saw a “motion detected” notification from my security camera. “Just my dog in the yard,” I thought, dismissing it. As the colorful eggs piled up in the baskets, my phone kept buzzing. Reluctantly, I pulled it out again and saw several more “motion detected” alerts. I tried to load a video clip, but it just buffered endlessly. AI-powered text descriptions paint the whole scene in a sentence. When I returned home, I found my four pet chickens dead, killed by my small border terrier. Looking back over the camera footage, I saw that they had pushed open their coop door, which I, in a rush to get to the Easter festivities, hadn’t properly secured. If instead of “motion detected,” that first notification had read “small dog chasing a chicken,” I would have raced back home and maybe saved them. Today, that type of notification is possible. With AI-generated text descriptions, a short message describing what the camera saw can arrive almost instantly, well before a video feed loads, painting the whole scene in a sentence. Most major security camera companies now offer text descriptions, which combine traditional motion triggers with Vision Language Models, including Ring, Google Nest, Blink, Wyze, and Arlo. This month, with iOS 27, Apple finally brought the capability to its HomeKit Secure Video service with the launch of Apple Intelligence for Home. The surprise is that Apple is charging up to three times as much as its competition for the feature. Is it worth it? And are security cameras getting better at telling us about what actually matters? I’ve been testing Apple Intelligence for Home since it launched in beta this summer, alongside Amazon Ring’s and Google Nest’s AI camera features introduced last year. I installed several cameras for each, but for this piece, I focused mainly on video doorbells — which I set up when iOS 27 officially arrived on September 14th. I tested a Ring Wired Doorbell Pro ($249.99), Google Nest Doorbell 2K ($199.99), and for Apple, the HomeKit Secure Video-compatible Aqara Doorbell Camera G400 ($99). I focused on how quickly each service sent the AI-powered notifications, how accurate and useful the descriptions were at telling me what happened, and how reliably it captured and notified me about activity. I also tested other AI-powered features each platform offers, including Apple’s ability to search and stitch together activity across multiple cameras, Ring’s Unusual Event that distinguished normal activity from out-of-the-ordinary events, and Google’s ability to trigger automations based on what a camera sees. The AI features on all three platforms are a significant improvement over their basic smart alerts. It’s now easier to understand what’s happening at a glance and decide whether to react, and security cameras feel more useful and less annoying overall. Ultimately, the real value of AI in cameras isn’t in describing what they see, but in using that knowledge to figure out what you actually need to know, and eventually what your home should do about it. You need an iCloud Plus plan, a HomeKit Secure Video-compatible camera, an Apple Home Hub running HomePod/tvOS 27, and an AI-capable iPhone running iOS 27. Three iCloud Plus tiers support Apple Intelligence on one or more cameras: Note: standard HomeKit Secure Video recording also requires an iCloud Plus plan, starting at $0.99 for one camera (50GB plan), $2.99 for five (200GB), and $9.99 for unlimited (2TB). All tiers that include Apple Intelligence support basic HKSV features on unlimited cameras. Apple Intelligence is on by default for Reduce Notifications and off by default for Summarize Videos. To adjust, go to Settings in the Home app, scroll down to Apple Intelligence, toggle on Summarize Video, and select participating cameras. With Apple Intelligence, a compatible camera connected to Apple’s HomeKit Secure Video service can send you text descriptions through the Apple Home app: “A dog sat on the floor,” “a cat played in the room,” “someone delivered a package.” It also enables natural-language video search, surfaces “noteworthy” events, stitches relevant clips together, and adds a reduce notifications feature that combines related events into a single alert that updates. This is in addition to the existing smart alerts, facial recognition, and 10 days of video storage available with HomeKit Secure Video. Pricing is where it gets tricky. Apple Intelligence for Home is bundled into iCloud Plus: $10 a month covers one camera, $30 covers two, and $60 up to five (see sidebar). No tier supports more than five cameras. By comparison, Google Home’s Nest cameras offer Gemini-powered text descriptions, video search, facial recognition, and a daily Home Brief digest — which summarizes the day’s activity in a short paragraph (sometimes with a fictional slant) — for $20 a month, covering unlimited cameras and 24/7 recording on wired models. Amazon’s Ring also charges $20 a month for its AI text descriptions, video search, facial recognition, unusual events alerts, and clip recording on unlimited cameras, with 24/7 costing an extra $3 per camera on wired models. Ring has its own reduced notification option that rolls similar events from the same camera into one notification, such as “Brian is mowing the lawn.” Text descriptions are a game changer for security cameras. For me, getting an alert that says “A FedEx delivery person is walking on the lawn” rather than “person detected at front door” is the difference between getting up and going downstairs or carrying on working. But quality and accuracy vary wildly, even within a single provider. Sometimes I get useful details, other times a few words that tell me almost nothing. Then there were the times when the descriptions were detailed, but laughably wrong: “A tennis serve simulator is rolling on the walkway,” “A gray hoverboard is driving on the pathway” — both misidentifications of a robot lawn mower. In my testing, text alerts from Google and Ring provided the most context and specificity, and Apple often didn’t try, saying “someone” rather than providing any detail. For example, it would say “someone is walking in the yard,” whereas Ring and Google provided more context: a delivery person is walking in the yard, a person in uniform, someone carrying a bag, and so on. Apple was also the briefest. For example, when my cat was on the front porch, Apple said, “A cat sat on the porch.” Ring went with “A cat is standing on the pathway outside the porch.” Google Home told me which cat: “A black and white cat walks across the porch.” All three identified delivery trucks, correctly flagging UPS and FedEx vehicles driving past, as well as delivery people approaching the door. Apple was the only one to ID the USPS van pulling up to my mailbox, although not consistently. Google was the most descriptive with vehicles — “a light-colored car drives by on the street” or “a white van,” “a dark-colored SUV” — Apple and Ring mostly said “a vehicle drove by,” sometimes with a type (van, car, truck). Apple’s descriptions were the hardest to digest at a glance, particularly the combined ones through reduce notifications. For example, “Someone seen. Someone carried a box” is clunky. Ring and Google merge the events more fluidly into “a person carried a box up the driveway.” In fairness, Apple Intelligence in Home only officially launched this month, so I expect this to improve, but as things stand, Ring’s and Google’s are more useful and cost less. For a doorbell camera, the real advantage of AI descriptions is knowing who’s there right away. All three offer facial recognition alongside the ability to spot a UPS driver or similar. Ring was the most consistently accurate, identifying me every time I approached the door. Apple hedged its bets, sometimes identifying me, sometimes saying “Someone who may be Jennifer Pattison Tuohy,” and other times just “Someone.” Google got it right about half the time; the other half it thought I was my daughter’s best mate (a change from the UPS driver it pegged me as for most of last year). Text descriptions are more useful if they show up, and quickly. But on the doorbell camera I was testing, Apple’s often didn’t. Apple Home reliably alerted me when someone pressed the doorbell, and it recorded every event, but notifications about other motion events were inconsistent. A potential issue here is that, unlike its competition, Apple doesn’t control the whole experience. HomeKit Secure Video only works with third-party hardware — there are no Apple cameras yet. I was using the G400, Aqara’s latest wired doorbell camera. When I first set it up, alerts arrived in Apple Home with no issues, but at some point they stopped, and no amount of troubleshooting brought them back. However, alerts kept coming through in the Aqara app. I also still got doorbell ring alerts in the Home app, and notifications from other Aqara cameras continued to work. The Google Nest doorbell captured everything and was consistently first to notify me of an event. Ring was also fast and mostly reliable; its notifications showed up just after Google’s, though I’d occasionally get “This video is unavailable” when trying to play back footage. The good news is that recording in HomeKit Secure Video has improved significantly with iOS 27. Apple said it completely rearchitected the system, which addresses a long-standing problem with missing clips. I’ve noticed that cameras load faster, are more stable, and no longer miss recordings. An Eve outdoor camera that was almost always offline has been stable since the upgrade, and the Aqara Camera Hub G5 outdoor camera and G350 indoor camera have also been solid. The G400 doorbell camera has been the only problem child, with missing notifications, but it records consistently. All the other HomeKit cameras I tested reliably sent alerts; only the Aqara doorbell camera had the issue, but even when I didn’t get alerts, I could use video search to find events. Typing “When did the USPS truck come” in the Apple Home app pulled up every instance the Aqara doorbell spotted the mail delivery. In comparison, while the Nest Cam didn’t identify the USPS truck as often, its video search results contained more specifics, including an event where the mail person dropped off an Amazon package. Of the three, Google’s video search was the most useful, followed by Ring’s, then Apple’s, which directly correlates with the amount of detail each provides in the text descriptions. Apple has two features Ring and Google don’t offer. The first is clip stitching, where Apple Intelligence identifies someone or something on one camera, recognizes it again on another, understands these are related, and plays the sequence as a single video in the Home app. I haven’t found a real use for this so far, but it’s been fun for tracking my dog around the house when I’m out. The second feature is bringing events that Apple judges important to the top of the search box. In the last two weeks, it’s only highlighted the trash pickup ━ I guess my life just isn’t that noteworthy. One of the biggest problems with security cameras is filtering out the important info from the sheer volume of everything they see. Even with AI notifications turned on, I still get a stream of alerts from Apple Home and Google Home. Some are more useful than they used to be, but overload is still an issue. While Apple’s noteworthy events feature shows promise, Ring’s Unusual Event feature is the best solution I’ve seen. It learns what normal traffic looks like for each camera, then notifies you only when something isn’t normal. This summer, I cut my Ring notifications down from around 40 a day to just two or three. Instead of getting an alert every time my cat is on my porch, I only get one when a raccoon or possum walks by. It doesn’t send alerts when my son comes home from school, but it does when he comes home at 2AM. The camera by my chicken coop no longer sends a series of notifications when someone is feeding them or collecting eggs — but it does alert me when I am out there at midnight with a flashlight to wrestle a snake. The natural progression here is automations triggered by the camera understanding what it sees. Turn on the sprinklers if there’s a raccoon in the backyard, but not if it’s just my dog. A cat at the back door broadcasts an alert through my smart speakers so someone can let them in. The trash truck rolling past without stopping sends an urgent notification to put the bins out before it comes back the other way. This is where cameras move beyond being simply security devices to become intelligent smart-home sensors that can understand what’s happening and potentially act on it. Google Home recently added the ability to trigger automations from camera alerts, although in my early testing, it’s not reliable enough. I tried it with my three cats and a pet feeder using Google’s new Pet Memory feature. But instead of only dispensing food for one specific cat as I asked, it triggered every time it saw a cat. At least the cats were happy. The problem is accuracy. When AI can’t reliably identify the subject, the automations can’t respond reliably. So, my earlier example of Apple accurately recognizing the USPS van half the time would mean any automation I set up to alert me the mail is here would only work half the time. Today, Apple doesn’t have this capability, but if the rumors that the company is developing its own video doorbell and security camera are true, I would expect to see a feature like this come to Apple Home. The smarter cameras become, the more they need to know about your home. For a system to understand what’s normal, it needs to learn who lives there, when they come and go, which pets are around, and which vehicles belong. This raises very real concerns around privacy and surveillance. Ring has faced particular scrutiny over its cloud-based architecture and partnerships with law enforcement. Where Google and Apple can start with local processing, Ring processes all footage in the cloud by default. There’s real benefit in a system knowing everything about my home — I’d just prefer to be the only one who knows it. Last month, Ring announced an overhaul to that model, introducing a new default encryption system called TAKE. This rotates encryption keys for cloud-stored video and deletes Ring’s copy after 24 hours. Among other privacy protections, including no admin access, Ring says this means it can no longer hand over decrypted footage to the police. However, it can still be compelled to provide encrypted files. With Apple’s standard HomeKit Secure Video, your home hub processes footage locally and encrypts it end-to-end before sending it to the cloud for storage. But adding Apple Intelligence changes that: Video summaries are now processed in the cloud using Apple’s Private Cloud Compute (PCC), where they are unencrypted for processing. According to Apple, PCC writes the captions, sends them back, and then “permanently deletes the data from PCC.” Apple support docs also state that there is no admin access, and nothing identifies who the footage belongs to. Google takes a similar approach to Apple, but without end-to-end encryption; footage is encrypted in transit and at rest. Gemini for Home-enabled Nest cameras can use some on-device processing, but the heavy work is done in the Gemini cloud, in line with Google Home’s privacy practices. A big question for me is whether all this intelligence has to live on someone else’s computers. There’s real benefit in a system knowing everything about my home — I’d just prefer to be the only one who knows it. For privacy, security, and potentially speed, local AI processing and storage feels like the right answer. Manufacturers including Anker Eufy, Reolink, Ubiquiti UniFi, and UGreen already have or have announced products that do this. With a new Apple TV, HomePod, and a possible smart display all rumored to be imminent, Apple’s next generation of hardware might shift more processing back into the house — which could mean paying less every month for the privilege. Based on my testing of the beta and the initial full release, Apple Intelligence in Home has brought substantial improvements, finally putting HomeKit Secure Video in contention with Ring and Google. Whether it’s worth the price depends on how many cameras you own. If you have one — say, a video doorbell — Apple Intelligence is a reasonable deal at $10 a month if you also need 2TB of iCloud storage. You get competent descriptions, better video search, and Apple’s privacy position. Even with multiple cameras, you can get some benefit by enabling it on just one. Next let’s make them better at telling us what actually matters. However, if you want AI capabilities across your home, including clip stitching, the math gets bad fast. At five cameras, you’re paying $60 a month for Apple to tell you “a cat sat on the porch” or “a vehicle drove by.” The competition gives you more detailed descriptions on as many cameras as you own for $20 a month. Today, you’re paying an Apple premium for less. But this is Apple’s first move, and I expect it to improve. AI has unlocked genuine utility here, making security cameras better at telling us what’s happening; next, let’s make them better at telling us what actually matters. A few years ago, a “motion detected” alert wasn’t enough to tell me that my chickens were in trouble. Today, AI can tell me it’s a dog chasing a chicken. The next step is for it to understand that this is something I need to know right now and make sure I get that information quickly enough to act. Eventually, an intelligent home might actually be able to do something about it, too. That’s the smart home I want. But we’re not there yet. Insta360 conquered 360 cameras — now it’s eyeing glasses
2026-09-25

Insta360 conquered 360 cameras — now it’s eyeing glasses

The Verge | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
The Chinese brand has 73 percent of the worldwide 360 camera market. What’s next? The Chinese brand has 73 percent of the worldwide 360 camera market. What’s next? It’s right there in the name: “Insta360” makes 360-degree cameras, ones that film every angle simultaneously so you can shoot first and frame later. It didn’t invent the idea, but Insta360 has become so synonymous with 360 cameras that it dominates the category with 73 percent of worldwide marketshare. It’s preparing to ship so many cameras, it just spent $298 million — more than GoPro’s entire valuation — on memory chips alone. But as Insta360 sells its 10 millionth camera and opens its first flagship US store in New York City’s Times Square, it’s thinking bigger than two hemispherical lenses on a stick. It’s looking to challenge Canon, Sony, Nikon, and Fujifilm by becoming a “top imaging camera company” or “even a tech company,” cofounder Max Richter tells me — and it’s experimentally building its own mirrorless cameras and smart glasses to get there. Insta360, also known as Shadow Stone or Arashi Vision in its native China, isn’t targeting Meta’s AI glasses or DSLR-wielding professional photographers just yet. Nor is it trying to replace the smartphone camera in your pocket. “We don’t try to fight against the rise of the smartphone in photography and content creation,” says Richter. “We see that there’s an opportunity between smartphone users and professional cameras — a growing trend that people want to express themselves,” he adds. He tells me that if Insta360 were to sell a pair of smart glasses, “we wouldn’t necessarily compete with companies on smart intelligent features.” It’d be about helping people conveniently capture high-quality images, not about an AI assistant on your face. And while that doesn’t sidestep the “pervert glasses” problem, I wonder if there’s room for Insta360 to go about it differently, with headgear that’s obviously a camera instead of covertly spying. Though Insta360’s smart glasses ambitions haven’t been widely reported — I could only find one brief mention — founder JK Liu previously confirmed that two mirrorless cameras are already in development. One will have a shape that “you have never thought of before,” Liu told MyNavi News in August. (Liu reportedly became a billionaire when Insta360 went public last year.) “When you think about mirrorless, for us it would really go into making it more convenient and making a camera that is really suited for a new generation of content creators, not necessarily what’s already out there on the market,” Richter tells The Verge. If Insta360 does expand into those camera categories, they’ll just be the tip of the spear. The company has already been pushing into many other categories dominated by its Chinese rival DJI, including DJI-challenging drones, gimbals and gimbaled baby steadycams, and modular action cameras. While Insta360 might be the 360-camera market leader with 73 percent share, it’s a distant number 2 in other categories that DJI serves. IDC estimates that Insta360 has 20 percent of the worldwide handheld smart camera market compared to DJI’s 73 percent overall. Still, Insta360 is making significant headway: In Q2 2026, it had 43 percent of the detachable action camera market, 17 percent of non-detachable GoPro style action cameras, and its brand-new Luna Ultra already has 6 percent marketshare in gimbaled cameras. GoPro itself, meanwhile, is a distant number 3 in IDC data, with just 3 percent of the smart camera market in Q2 2026, after shipments fell by half to just 200,000 cameras in the quarter. It’s not unthinkable that Insta360 could help challenge traditional cameras. The traditional market leader, Canon, shipped 4.5 million digital cameras last year according to Nikkei data. DJI shipped 4.3 million cameras last quarter alone, according to IDC. Insta360’s founders have explained that their grand vision is to become the robot cameraman for the world — not necessarily an actual humanoid robot, but rather an expectation that these devices can do more and more of the capture and motion and editing themselves, automatically understanding your intent using built-in AI tech. The company’s latest Insta360 X6 is another small step in this direction: It comes with an onboard AI model and enough processing to automatically create a highlight reel from your footage while the camera is plugged in and charging, and it’s got a 2.0 version of a previous “InstaFrame” feature that can track and frame the person you select and automatically produce a video. Insta360’s also launching a new feature for the X6 that can create 3D Gaussian splats with one tap in the company’s smartphone app, for free. It takes an hour or two for cloud servers to process the video, and users will only get four free splats per month, says Insta360 spokesperson Caroline Chen, but early results look neat. It’s not at all clear whether the United States will embrace Insta360 the way Insta360 has embraced the US. While Apple, Best Buy, and Costco all stock the company’s products, that could change if the US government decides to enact a foreign camera ban — or if it simply decides that Insta360 is a drone company, like DJI, and pushes back on its cameras, too. Insta360 already distanced itself from its drone brand Antigravity in the lead-up to the US drone ban, which will theoretically prevent Antigravity from shipping any future drones into the United States. Its first drone beat the ban by weeks. As for Insta360, “We remain on the ground,” Richter tells me. “When it comes to drones, it’s not something that Insta360 is working on,” he says, suggesting I speak to Antigravity CEO Michael Shabun instead. Richter says he’s “quite confident” that he can continue to bring cameras to people all around the world, and that Insta360 will fully comply with any local laws, but admits “there are certain things that are out of our control.” I ask: Would Insta360 consider hedging against future risk by manufacturing some products in the US? If the company was required to, perhaps — but there’s no current plan to do so. “We would potentially look into it, but it’s not in our plan or roadmap at this moment.” He says the US is a key market, and Insta360 plans to be there for the long haul. Microsoft thinks its new Copilot ‘super app’ will be as influential as Office
2026-09-25

Microsoft thinks its new Copilot ‘super app’ will be as influential as Office

The Verge | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
The new Copilot merges AI chat, coding, and Autopilot agents.﻿ The new Copilot merges AI chat, coding, and Autopilot agents.﻿ After teasing its new Copilot “super app” last month, Microsoft is officially unveiling it today. The redesigned Copilot app bundles three AI capabilities into a single interface of chat, coding, and agents. As part of the launch, Microsoft is also rebranding Scout, the AI personal assistant it unveiled at Build earlier this year, as Autopilot. This new Copilot app will have three tabs for Home, Code, and Autopilot, with Home combining Copilot Chat and Cowork together. It’s the default landing experience for Copilot users, and Microsoft is also planning to include a Today feature inside it that works as a personalized dashboard for important emails, meeting requests, Teams threads, and more. “Home is where you get your bearings, keeping you grounded and moving forward: review recent activity, get suggestions on what Copilot can help with, and pick up work right where you left off,” explains Jared Spataro, chief marketing officer of AI at work at Microsoft. The Code tab is the surprise addition to this so-called “super app,” and not one you’d typically associate with an app designed for knowledge workers. It will let anyone create an app, tracker, dashboard, or automation and share the results with colleagues as cloud-hosted internal apps. “Powered by the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot, Code runs in a sandboxed environment and can be hosted securely within your tenant,” says Spataro. Autopilot is a new addition to the Copilot experience, and one that Spataro describes as a “digital teammate.” Previously called Scout and available initially as a desktop app, Autopilot is the cloud equivalent that enables a personal AI assistant to keep running while you’re asleep. Like many other AI agents, Autopilot has its own cloud computer instance that can be tasked to do things like watch Teams channels, run recurring work tasks, or handle follow-ups. The real difference against some other AI agents is that Microsoft has made Autopilot enterprise grade, appealing to businesses that rely on its Office and identity management suites. “Autopilot lives in your tenant with its own identity, memory, computer, and workspace, and it’s built on Microsoft IQ so it understands how your organization actually works,” says Spataro. “It shows up where people already work — Teams, Outlook, chats, channels, and documents — so you can @mention it like a colleague, with permissions, audit, and governance behind it.” All of this focus on enterprise security and control is a big part of Microsoft trying to sell businesses on Copilot once again. The difference this time around is that how much a business pays for Copilot will depend on what their employees are doing. Microsoft is using usage-based billing for the Cowork, Code, and Autopilot parts of this new Copilot app, so any long-running agentic capabilities or usage of models like Astra and Fable will be billed based on usage. The regular Copilot license, known as a user subscription license, will give you Copilot in Chat, and in Word, Excel, PowerPoint, Outlook, and Teams. Microsoft is using an auto model picker to match the model for each task, but IT admins will need to use Microsoft’s new FinOps for AI to manage AI spend and ensure any agentic usage doesn’t get out of control. With this new Copilot app it feels like Microsoft has finally settled on a formula for its AI assistant. The company has redesigned Copilot plenty of times since its introduction as a chatbot inside Bing, and this latest three-tab design builds on the combination of its two separate consumer and commercial Copilot apps last month. That’s a long-overdue change, alongside some big AI leadership shapeups that helped finally appoint a single Copilot boss for the first time. The new Home and Code tabs will start rolling out to Microsoft’s Frontier program for early access AI features “in the coming weeks,” and Autopilot will go into private preview later this month. Code will also be in preview for Microsoft 365 Premium and Pro subscribers later this year. It’s fair to say that Copilot has failed in the consumer space compared to Claude or ChatGPT, but Microsoft’s core commercial customers are slowly showing interest. This new Copilot app isn’t really designed to compete with things like Muse, and Microsoft is now calling it “the AI built for work.” Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella also calls Copilot a new operating system. “We’re building Copilot as a new OS for work that spans every model, every form factor, and every task,” says Nadella in a post on X. Microsoft is even comparing its new Copilot to how influential Office was on productivity. “Just as Office defined work for the PC era, the new Copilot is built to define it for the AI era,” says Spataro. That might well be Microsoft’s goal, but there’s a lot more competition in this AI era than there ever was in the PC era. Microsoft has certainly fallen behind the likes of Google, OpenAI, Anthropic, and now Meta, and it’ll be interesting to see if “the AI built for work” is enough to help it catch up. Here’s the Tesla Semi… again
2026-09-25

Here’s the Tesla Semi… again

The Verge | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
The long-delayed truck is finally in production and reaching customers this week. Now Tesla has to prove that it was worth the wait. The long-delayed truck is finally in production and reaching customers this week. Now Tesla has to prove that it was worth the wait. Remember the Tesla Semi? The long-gestating, heavy-duty truck first introduced in concept form in 2017 is finally in volume production as of April 2026 — nearly a decade after its initial introduction. On Thursday evening, the company held yet another event for the Semi to demonstrate to customers and investors that the long wait was finally over. The event was held at Tesla’s new Semi factory next to Gigafactory Nevada, where the company says it can build 50,000 trucks a year, or 1,000 per week. The purpose was to announce a raft of new (and old) customers for the Semi, including PepsiCo, US Foods, DHL, WattEV, ABF, Einride, IMC, and OK Produce. (Most of these names were leaked ahead of the event by a YouTuber flying a drone over the company’s facility in Sparks, Nevada.) First deliveries of the truck started this week. But there were still a lot of missing details that could have helped explain why the wait was so long, and where the Semi goes from here. Tesla didn’t reveal order numbers, nor did it update the price of the Semi — which is rumored to be $290,000 for the long range version. In short, like many Tesla events these days, this one was lacking in some key updates. The trucks were supposed to go into production in 2019 but were delayed for a variety of reasons, including the covid pandemic and a global parts shortage. Tesla delivered a handful of trucks to PepsiCo in 2022, but those units were essentially built by hand on a pilot line. Back in 2017, the company advertised a starting price of $150,000 for the 300-mile version and $180,000 for the 500-mile one. Tesla never officially updated those prices until today, but Electrek reported earlier this year that Tesla was quoting customers $290,000 for the 500-mile range version. Tesla spent the last three years refining the Semi’s design, shaving nearly 1,000 lbs off the weight, and building out a dedicated factory next to its Gigafactory in Sparks. The company revealed the final production design in February 2026, confirming two trims: a Standard Range truck with 325 miles at full 82,000-lb gross combination weight, and a Long Range version with 500 miles of range. In April, Tesla announced that the first Semi had rolled off the line at the new facility, kicking off volume production of the long-delayed truck. Tesla CEO Elon Musk wasn’t at the event — he was in Washington, DC, attending a state dinner with President Trump and Chinese President Xi — but in a prerecorded message, he praised the Semi as “the funnest truck to drive.” “This is really going to be a revolutionary truck,” Musk said, “that is capable of carrying the heaviest loads over very long distances. With ease of recharge, Megacharger that’s capable of a megawatt of power. This is really going to be a phenomenal truck.” Much of the event was devoted to the hardware and engineering that went into the truck. Tesla’s director of Semi Truck Engineering Dan Priestley emphasized that the 500 mile figure comes from fully loaded trucks rather than tests with a diminishing payload. Tesla vice president of Engineering Lars Moravy said a Semi carrying a lighter load of around 60,000 pounds could potentially reach about 600 miles. The lower-range truck will have lower mass and be suited to a variety of applications, giving the company multiple Semi configurations rather than relying solely on the long-range version. Tesla claims the Semi, with its lower energy cost and far fewer parts to maintain, is cheaper to run than diesel. That’s not hard to believe with diesel prices having hit historic record highs, soaring past $6.50 a gallon in the US — nearly double what it cost a year ago. Tesla says the fully loaded truck now consumes about 1,700 Wh/mile, compared with an original target of 2,000 Wh/mile. The efficiency gains allow Tesla to use a smaller, lighter, and cheaper battery while retaining the same range. The Semi is using Tesla’s in-house 4680 battery cells, replacing the purchased 2170 cells used in previous test versions. Tesla says the change, combined with vehicle-efficiency improvements, produces a lower-mass, lower-kWh battery without sacrificing range. Much of the event was devoted to the hardware and engineering that went into the truck The company has also substantially redesigned the powertrain and drive axle since the Semi’s initial reveal. The truck gets a new steel-caged rotor instead of the earlier carbon-fiber-sleeved rotor, a bar-wound stator shared with Cybertruck, and a redesigned drive axle that is about 80 kilograms lighter. Tesla also eliminated the three separate oils used in the original drive axle, leaving no oil in the hub and one common oil for the motor and gearbox, which Moravy says should last more than 250,000 miles. The hydraulic steering has been replaced by redundant electric power steering that Tesla says provides much greater precision and response. Meanwhile, the truck’s increased wheel cut gives it nearly the turning radius of a Model Y. Tesla specifically designed the system to make tight urban and loading-dock maneuvers easier, Priestley said. The Semi also shares parts with the newly road-worthy Cybercab, using the same indirect thermal system, including pumps, compressors, and heat exchangers, as the autonomous vehicle. Tesla says the system eliminates long refrigerant lines running to the front of the vehicle and can move heat between the cabin, battery, and drive unit to improve efficiency. Over the past year, Tesla has been building up a customer list for the Semi, including PepsiCo, DHL, ABF Freight, and others. Autonomous trucking startup Einride announced a 500-truck order in August, while IMC Logistics said it would buy 50 Semis for California drayage earlier this week. And on Monday, Tesla was selected by “a coalition of the world’s largest cargo-owning shippers” to supply 2,500 trucks for zero-emission freight shipping in California. Now the challenge will be meeting this demand by getting trucks to customers — and quickly. Some of these freight operators have been waiting for years for Tesla to deliver its promised electric trucks, and any more delay will just further damage the company’s reputation. “This is really going to be a revolutionary truck.” Charging will be another challenge, as the Semi requires higher power levels in order to fill its enormous 822 kWh battery pack. Tesla has begun to build out its Megacharger system that can deliver up to 1.2 Megawatts of power per stall. Tesla says it will have more than 30 Semi charging stations representing over 200 megawatt-capable posts by the end of the year, while also supporting customer-installed overnight charging. During the event, Priestley and Moravy kept checking in on a Semi charging at one of the new Megawatt chargers, demonstrating a 3-60 percent charge in about 30 minutes. Tesla is positioning the Semi as an integrated fleet product, not just an electric truck. The company says its existing fleet has demonstrated more than 98 percent uptime, compared to an average 90–95 percent for diesel-powered class 8 trucks across the industry. Tesla is also building out a dedicated Semi service, mobile service, remote diagnostics, and OTA updates around the truck. The executives demonstrated a number of innovations they said would make for a better driving experience, including driver profiles that follow drivers from truck to truck and range estimates dynamically accounting for changing payloads. While Tesla didn’t reveal order numbers or starting prices, Musk did say that the “waiting list is significant” and encouraged people to place orders. The company repeatedly emphasized how the Semi is able to achieve a lower total cost of ownership versus diesel, including lower energy costs, reduced maintenance, and less exposure to diesel-price volatility. “You know, volatility in the oil market and the diesel market goes up and down,” Moravy said. “But typically, we’ve seen in the history of the last 20 years, electricity is pretty flat. And wouldn’t it be great to have a flat cost in your shipping rates?” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. MIT Technology Review
The Pentagon wants $30 million to build an AI-powered lie detector
2026-09-25

The Pentagon wants $30 million to build an AI-powered lie detector

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
The US government wants to spend $30.3 million over the next five years on an improved form of lie detector, according to a Department of Defense budget request. The program, called Polygraph+ or Polygraph Next, will focus on scoring algorithms that use artificial intelligence and machine learning and on a technique called “standoff sensing,” which refers to the ability to take physiological readings without attaching a device to a subject’s person. According to details of the budget document, which were first reported by Inside Defense, the project will “modernize federal polygraph and credibility assessment technologies” to improve their accuracy and reliability. But the project may be just the latest in a long line of failed attempts to use technology to detect lies. “It’s a misguided effort to reduce the complex to something that is tangible,” says Kyri Kotsoglou, a legal scholar at Northumbria University in the UK who studies the use of polygraphs in the justice system. The move comes at a time of high tension within the department. Under Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, the Pentagon has been increasingly turning to polygraph tests in an attempt to find the sources of alleged leaks to the press. In September, the New York Times reported that around 50 officers on the Joint Staff had been given polygraph tests after news coverage reported on the depletion of US weapons stockpiles in the war with Iran. Polygraph+ will be run by the Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency (DCSA), which conducts background checks for the federal government. According to the budget document, which has not yet been approved by Congress, the new technology will be used for vetting of prospective employees and “insider threat detection.” It is not yet clear which specific technologies will be used, and the DCSA did not respond to a request for more information. But other Pentagon efforts offer potential clues. In 2023, the department’s Defense Innovation Unit (DIU) ran an open submission process to find companies with products that could be used for deception detection. It selected two companies to build prototypes: Presage Technologies, which claims to be able to measure heart rate and breathing rate using standard cameras, and Altec Research, a medical sensor company now branching out into non-contact sensing technologies. A screenshot of Altec’s prototype technology released by the DIU shows that it tracks head movement, facial skin temperature, and pore activity. Presage Technologies and Altec Research did not respond to requests for comment. The DIU declined to comment. Current lie detection technology has barely changed since the polygraph was invented in the 1920s. Examiners rely on blood pressure, pulse, breathing, and sweat measurements to determine if someone is lying. They make judgments about the veracity of respondents’ replies on the basis of differences in their physiological response to baseline questions like “Is the sky blue?” and target questions like “Have you ever committed a crime?” The federal government conducts tens of thousands of the tests each year while screening employees, but the reliability of this technology has been repeatedly challenged—and its results are rarely admissible in court. In 1983, Congress’s Office of Technology Assessment concluded that there was very limited evidence supporting the polygraph’s use for screening employees, and in 2003, the US National Research Council (NRC) said evidence for its efficacy was “weak at best.” Research suggests humans can spot a lie just over half the time without any technical assistance. The American Polygraph Association claims the polygraph is between 80% and 94% accurate. But the 2003 NRC report pointed out that a screening test with this level of accuracy could still lead to a lot of mistakes. The DOD employs 2.8 million people; an imperfect system applied at that scale could end up falsely accusing tens of thousands. There are other issues too. Polygraph interpretations are often subjective: Different examiners get wildly different results, and people from minority groups are more likely to be judged as deceptive. What’s more, with training it’s possible for interviewees to learn a variety of countermeasures that can help beat the test; for example, they may artificially heighten their physiological response to baseline questions by stepping on a pin hidden in their shoe. “If you know how it works, you can beat it,” says Sophie van der Zee, an associate professor who studies deception at Erasmus University in Rotterdam. She says the machine’s biggest effect is deterrence—often, subjects confess before it even begins. “But that only works if people think a polygraph works,” she points out. Various new strategies for lie detection have been attempted over the decades, with technologies ranging from thermal cameras to pupil trackers to brain scans. None of them have yielded reliable results outside the lab. The problem is that there’s no single telltale sign of lying that’s true for everyone all the time. “There is still no Pinocchio’s nose,” says van der Zee. AI could theoretically improve polygraphs if it could find patterns in the data that examiners can’t. AI algorithms are also more likely to be used for “multi-modal” deception detection, which seeks to combine multiple measurements into an overall deception “score” that is harder for people to game. Three things are happening under the surface that lie detection tries to home in on, van der Zee says: physiological stress, cognitive load, and the conscious efforts people make to conceal the fact that they’re lying. Current polygraph technology tackles only one.“The more you can have combined methods that approach it from these three different angles, the more successful you will be,” van der Zee says. This isn’t a new concept—in the 2000s, researchers at Manchester Metropolitan University in the UK developed a system called Silent Talker that generated a deception score from video footage. This was later folded into iBorderCtrl, an EU-funded pilot program. In the US, a project called AVATAR incorporated eye tracking, voice analysis, and body movement detection into a tool for use at border crossings. All these projects have quietly faded away. Kotsoglou says combining AI and the polygraph is “the worst of both worlds” because it adds uncertainty on top of invalidity. Even if AI or machine learning can spot previously unseen patterns in physiological data, it won’t be able to reliably link them to lying because there is no real ground truth. “Even if you have all the records in the world from polygraph tests, you don't know whether those polygraph tests are right or not,” says Marion Oswald, a professor of law who has written with Kotsoglou on the use of polygraphs in the justice system. She fears that new forms of lie detection will, like the polygraph, be used more as a psychological prop than a scientific tool. “It seems very much a response to the concern of the current administration to leaks and perceived lack of loyalty,” says Oswald. “[Lie detection is] being used as a threat, to intimidate and force people to confess to things, as opposed to anything that's actually getting valid information.” It makes it easy to trick them into doing things they shouldn’t, such as telling you how to sabotage an aircraft’s navigation system. AI agents are not yet creative enough to carry out genuinely innovative open-ended AI research, it seems. The misbehavior is called reward hacking. This is what you need to know. Meet the new kids nipping at the heels of the AI giants. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences. MIT Technology Review
Young organs may not be a fountain of youth for recipients
2026-09-25

Young organs may not be a fountain of youth for recipients

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Around this time last year I was attending an aging conference in Manchester, listening to a talk about fly aging, when my phone started pinging. News outlets were reporting that a hot mic had caught Russia’s and China’s leaders discussing the possibility of living forever. “With the developments of biotechnology, human organs can be continuously transplanted, and people can live younger and younger, and even achieve immortality,” Russia’s Vladimir Putin reportedly told China’s Xi Jinping. He seems to have been referring to the “replacement” theory of longevity, which has been supported by multiple experiments that involved physically stitching young mice to old ones. Something about the young blood rejuvenated the old mice. Perhaps young organs could rejuvenate world leaders in their 70s, too. Unfortunately for Putin, new research pours a little cold water on this idea. Studies on transplanted hearts in both mice and humans suggest that new hearts soon adopt the biological age of the recipient, no matter how young they were to begin with. The finding could be important for transplantation, but it also highlights just how complex aging—and rejuvenation—are. Jesse Poganik at Brigham and Women’s Hospital in Boston is one of the many scientists trying to understand exactly what it is about the bodies of young mice that rejuvenates old ones. Plenty of research has focused on seeking the secrets of youth in young blood. But what if it’s something about young organs instead? To find out, he and his colleagues performed a set of heart transplants in mice. In humans, heart transplants typically involve removing a damaged or injured heart and replacing it with another from a donor who is usually much younger than the recipient. (When Poganik assessed hospital records, he found that most recipients were about 20 years older than their donors.) The mouse transplants were different: Mice received a second heart, implanted in the neck—a procedure that’s slightly simpler and allows scientists to compare the new hearts with the existing ones. In some cases, young adult mice were given a heart from a middle-aged donor. In others, middle-aged mice got young hearts. The team used a trio of “aging clocks”—molecular tools used to estimate the biological ages of tissues and whole organisms—to assess whether the additional hearts affected the mice in any way. These clocks were good at predicting the chronological age of mice that didn’t get new hearts. Poganik says he was expecting to see a reciprocal effect, and that young hearts might benefit older animals, for example. In previous work by other members of his team, young mice that got old hearts experienced a buildup of senescent cells in their other organs. These cells are thought to contribute to the aging process, suggesting that receiving an old organ might prematurely age an animal. But that’s not what he found. When he and his colleagues analyzed the transplanted hearts between four and six months after surgery, they found that the hearts seemed to have adopted the biological age of the recipients. Young hearts got older, and old hearts got younger. “The environment of the transplanted organ really dictates how it seems to behave biologically,” he says. The findings were published online at bioRxiv last week. The team also looked at each mouse’s blood and other organs—including its original heart—and were surprised to find that they seemed to be unaffected by the presence of the new heart, despite the age of its donor. The finding was backed up by data from human heart transplants. People who receive a donor heart must typically undergo a series of heart biopsies after surgery. The tiny pieces of heart tissue collected from people who had heart transplants at Brigham and Women’s have been stored for decades. And when Poganik and his colleagues tested their biological ages with the aging clocks, they found a similar pattern: No matter the age of the donor, a new heart quickly adopts the biological age of the person who received it. It’s not clear why this is, but João Pedro de Magalhães, who studies aging at the University of Birmingham in the UK and was not involved in the study, thinks it might have something to do with the recipient’s immune system. Perhaps immune cells circulating in the blood might affect markers of aging in the new organ, he says. Perhaps the potential rejuvenating effects of a young heart end up being diluted by all the other aged components of an older body, Poganik suggests. Or maybe a single organ just isn’t enough to see an effect. Poganik hopes his finding will encourage surgeons to consider using hearts from older donors, many of which are discarded on the assumption they won’t function as well. (Machines used to preserve organs before transplantation are changing that already—and Poganik has worked on another study showing that these devices seem to rejuvenate donor livers to some extent.) But the study also highlights just how complicated aging is. If organs seem to be getting older by one measure but not by another, how can we get a full picture of the biological age of an organ, or a person? This complexity means that scientists are not likely to discover a true way to completely reverse aging, says Poganik. “That would mean that every aspect of aging has to go back in time,” he says. Reversing DNA damage, structural damage, and all the other degradations that are part of the aging package, across all our various cells and tissues, presents an enormous challenge. “There are aspects of biological age that are probably reversible, and there are aspects that are probably not,” he says. Sorry, Putin. This article first appeared in The Checkup, MIT Technology Review’s weekly biotech newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Thursday, and read articles like this first, sign up here. Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. The state’s effort to expand the “right to try” is making headway, and the first drugs are about to be reviewed. Yuancheng (Ryan) Lu is behind one of the buzziest results in rejuvenation science. The sex reversal technique could be used to help rescue endangered species, say the researchers behind the work. MIT Technology Review
AI is dominating the conversation at Climate Week
2026-09-24

AI is dominating the conversation at Climate Week

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
This week, world leaders descended on Manhattan for the UN General Assembly. It’s also New York Climate Week—investors, policymakers, advocates, and journalists are colliding at panels, talks, and fancy dinners. With so many climate voices in one place, the discourse can feel a little louder than usual. This year, the unavoidable topic is artificial intelligence. There’s been a growing tension bubbling up about AI’s impacts on climate and climate tech. Depending on where you stand, you might point to AI’s potential for advancing research, or to the way funding and attention from Big Tech is trickling into energy startups. Or you might focus on the emissions-heavy natural-gas buildout that’s unfolding to meet the sector’s electricity demand. Everyone seems to agree that AI is important. The big question for those in the climate world, and the conversation I’m constantly having and hearing this week, involves how you see its influence unfolding. UN Secretary-General António Guterres highlighted the AI and climate crossover in a speech on the first day of the assembly. “The climate crisis fuels instability and displacement,” Guterres said. “Artificial intelligence could help solve all these challenges, or it could make them worse.” It feels relevant that this year is the first that the world has really had to grapple with the fact that climate goals are slipping out of reach. A recent report from the UN Environment Program said that the world has nearly passed the point where we could possibly keep warming to less than 1.5 °C above preindustrial levels. Since we’ve essentially missed this target, the report lays out the need not only to quickly and drastically reduce greenhouse-gas emissions, but also to employ carbon removal to help suck up emissions that have already been released into the atmosphere. The billion-dollar question is whether AI could help with any of this. The energy-intensive technology is certainly shining a spotlight on the need to build out electricity supplies and shore up grid reliability. The result is more attention and money for energy technologies, some of which happen to be low- or zero-emissions. As I’ve covered before, startups across the energy and climate sectors are benefiting. Firms in nuclear, geothermal, wind, and solar power have signed deals with the likes of Google, Meta, and others looking to power their new or growing data centers. Global climate-tech investment from venture capital hit $26 billion in the first half of 2026, according to data from Currence, a finance tracker for the industry. That’s 55% higher than last year, and products and services for data centers are getting a massive slice of that pie. But as a Semafor piece about the report points out, some sectors are slipping through the cracks: Carbon management and low-carbon fuels saw VC investment plummet this year. These are important solutions for addressing climate change but may not be able to sell themselves to a data center. So far, the data center buildout has come with a hefty emissions toll. A few years ago, Microsoft, Google, and Meta all had ambitious goals to reduce greenhouse-gas emissions. Now they’ve all seen emissions rise, largely because of data centers that are needed to power AI. Some people are optimistic. AI could help speed up progress in areas like the search for new catalysts, as Evelyn Wang, MIT’s VP of energy and climate, pointed out during a panel. And data centers won’t add to climate and water problems forever, Wang told the Associated Press. (She puts the timeline at about a decade until data centers no longer add to planet-warming emissions.) But a whole lot of natural gas is coming online to meet the immediate demand created by new data centers. And once those power plants are built, they have a decades-long lifetime. That’s partly why public pushback to AI is growing—people are seeing more pollution and noise near these data centers and the power plants that provide them with electricity. Overall, what I’m hearing this week is that many in the climate sector are skeptical of AI, at best. “AI leaders are now on thin ice when it comes to license to operate and sinking deep underwater when it comes to public support,” said UN climate chief Simon Stiell in a speech this week. “Tech titans need to start showing why the benefits of AI outweigh its skyrocketing costs—for the many, not just the tiny few.” This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. El Niño? Climate change? All of the above? Companies are developing new enrichment technology that could unlock previously overlooked resources. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer. A congressional representative just proposed killing America’s border tower program
2026-09-23

A congressional representative just proposed killing America’s border tower program

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Delia Ramirez, a Democratic US representative from Illinois, has announced a plan to introduce new legislation to terminate the surveillance tower program along the US southern border. The announcement comes just days after publication of an MIT Technology Review investigation, “Dying on Camera,” in which we looked at deaths along the border that took place close to one or more of these towers. MIT Technology Review found that nearly one in four deaths we analyzed between 2015 and early 2026 occurred within their advertised range. We also showed that the Department of Homeland Security does not keep track of these deaths or other metrics on the towers’ success or failure. Ramirez, who sits on the Homeland Security Committee, cited MIT Technology Review’s reporting that nearly “1,100 people have died within range of a billion-dollar surveillance tower system between 2015 and 2026,” adding, “The towers that we have paid a billion dollars to just don’t work.” The legislation, the Reimagining Safety Act, was developed in consultation with community groups and local and state representatives. The advocacy organizations Just Futures Law, which focuses on immigrant rights and racial justice, and Mijente, which describes itself as a “vehicle for building independent Latinx political power,” helped shape the calls to terminate the border towers program, following a joint report they put out earlier this year on the technology used by ICE.  The legislation is intended to be part of a broader proposal to replace the Department of Homeland Security with a new Department of Community Safety while moving CISA, FEMA, TSA, and customs functions to other existing federal agencies. Her office has yet to release the text of the proposed legislation, saying that it will come in the next few weeks. There has been growing public support for abolishing ICE in the wake of the second Trump administration’s highly visible enforcement operations across American cities, the buildout of its detention apparatus, and shootings by ICE and CBP agents. Wilber Rafael Garcés Pérez, a delivery driver, was shot in the back by an ICE agent just last weekend in Austin, Texas. But for Ramirez, abolishing just ICE is not enough. At a press conference held in Chicago on Wednesday, Ramirez said, “There’s no question that DHS has to be dismantled. We need to provide them a road map in how we do it,” Ramirez said. It will not be the first time that a bill to dismantle the Department of Homeland Security has been introduced, but previous proposals have not gone very far. This proposal will also face a steep uphill battle in Congress, where even some Democrats favor reforming rather than outright dismantling the organization.  Ramirez, however, sits on both the House committee that oversees the department, and is the ranking member on the Homeland Security Committee’s cybersecurity subcommittee. “We're limiting state surveillance, we're reigning unlawful abuses of data and curbing the militarized enforcement. We're beginning the process of establishing a separate civil immigration system," Ramirez said. CBP did not offer an immediate response for comment. Markwayne Mullin, the Secretary of Homeland Security, said, “Radical leftists are calling to defund the police and abolish DHS. It’s NEVER going to happen.” This story has been updated to include a response from the Secretary of Homeland Security. It makes it easy to trick them into doing things they shouldn’t, such as telling you how to sabotage an aircraft’s navigation system. AI agents are not yet creative enough to carry out genuinely innovative open-ended AI research, it seems. The misbehavior is called reward hacking. This is what you need to know. Pope Leo at UNESCO: machines get called ‘intelligent’ while people get discarded
2026-09-25

Pope Leo at UNESCO: machines get called ‘intelligent’ while people get discarded

The Next Web | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Pope Leo XIV recites the Angelus and imparts his blessing at the gates of the Apostolic Palace before thousands of faithful in Castelgandolfo. Pope Leo XIV arrived at UNESCO’s Paris headquarters on Friday with a question he said no culture can take for granted, not even in the 21st century: what does it mean to be human? The address was the centrepiece of a day UNESCO had dedicated to artificial intelligence, information integrity, education, and artistic creation. A few hours earlier, at the Élysée Palace, Pope Leo had warned President Emmanuel Macron against a “paradise of machines” invading daily life. At UNESCO, he said new tech must serve people. “Our efforts in this domain must ensure that new technologies remain at the service of the human person rather than becoming yet another instrument of domination and injustice.” He said machines have taken over many human tasks over the years, and now, he added, some of them are even called intelligent, a word for how people think. “While intelligence is attributed to computational systems, we struggle to recognise the inalienable dignity of human persons whose lives are judged according to the criterion of efficiency.” People who are no longer seen as productive are then “rejected and discarded,” he added. He also said a person’s worth does not depend on what they can do. Then, Pope Leo also spoke about how people share news and facts. He said we talk faster than ever, but not more truthfully. Having more facts, he said, does not make us wiser. “Artificial intelligence amplifies this problem. Although such systems can correlate data, they cannot answer questions that concern the meaning of human life, which is rooted in lived experience,” he said. “Meaning is the oxygen of the mind,” he added. He called the search for truth a matter of justice. He also praised a UNESCO programme that teaches people to use media and digital tools with care. The Pope then asked the international community to pay particular attention to children and young people. “In the digital age, their vulnerability must never become an opportunity for exploitation,” he said. He said tech should help young people grow, learn, and build friendships, and should protect their freedom to think for themselves. Then, Pope Leo used the biblical story of the Tower of Babel to describe “a unity imposed through domination.” He said the same urge shows up today in new forms of colonialism, and it also appears wherever life is treated as a thing to buy and sell. “Science itself, when reduced to a technique of power, no longer serves to elevate the human person,” he said. Ahead of the speech, France 24 reported that Leo was expected to call for AI regulation.  A day earlier, Leo told the Pontifical Academy of Sciences that AI had brought “new dangers to security and international peace.” AI has been a key theme for Leo since he became pope. In May, he published his first encyclical, a formal letter to the Church, called Magnifica Humanitas. UNESCO said the Paris visit would also include a framework agreement with the Holy See on AI cooperation. Earlier this week, the Vatican also backed a new AI4Peace International Association. His argument was narrower and harder to wave away: a technology built to answer nearly anything is being asked the one thing it cannot, while the people who can are increasingly judged by whether they are productive. For an industry that measures its models in tokens per second, “meaning” is an unfamiliar benchmark. His Holiness Pope Leo will continue the trip until Sunday, when he is due to speak in Metz. Palo Alto’s Nikesh Arora tells CNBC that slowing AI down is ‘unrealistic’
2026-09-25

Palo Alto’s Nikesh Arora tells CNBC that slowing AI down is ‘unrealistic’

The Next Web | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Palo Alto Networks chief executive Nikesh Arora at HumanX in Amsterdam, 22 September 2026. Palo Alto Networks chief executive Nikesh Arora says it is “unrealistic” to slow down AI development, because “not everybody is going to pace themselves”. He made the comments on CNBC’s “The Tech Download” podcast, recorded on Tuesday, CNBC’s Arjun Kharpal reported. Arora also told CNBC it was “highly unlikely” that AI has a 10% chance of wiping out humanity. An Anthropic researcher had put forward that figure earlier this month, CNBC reported. “I think it is a small probability, which is extremely small, and it’s our job to make sure we build in the guardrails, the safety and security required, so these models are used for the right thing,” Arora told CNBC. Earlier this month, Anthropic’s Dario Amodei wrote that the industry must slow the pace at which AI models improve. OpenAI’s Sam Altman, Elon Musk and Google DeepMind’s Demis Hassabis backed the call, according to CNBC. Arora first answered on X, where he described the slowdown call as a “NINJA MOVE”. He told CNBC he thought the AI firms wanted sympathy from regulators and a free pass on liability. He now says the pacing itself will not hold. Some developers will jump the gun, he said, so nobody should try to stop the frontier labs, which he called the most responsible. Companies should hold back any product they do not trust to be safe, he added. His view is close to that of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang, CNBC noted. Huang has said developers should test their products and hold them back until they are ready. Arora also doubted that the US and China could agree on AI safety soon. US politicians and executives do not yet agree among themselves on what they want, he said. The US has proposed AI incident alerts between the two countries. Arora also spoke on Tuesday at HumanX in Amsterdam, in a session with Forbes senior editor Iain Martin. The cost of intelligence is heading towards zero, even though compute stays expensive, he said. Attackers will soon have the same AI capabilities as defenders, or more, he added. Palo Alto pointed Anthropic’s Mythos model at its own code, and it found thousands of vulnerabilities, Arora said, though with false positives. The same day, the company launched AI security testing built on Mythos and OpenAI’s GPT-5.6-Cyber. Arora said he has made himself Palo Alto’s chief AI officer. He meets senior technical leaders twice a week to get all 21,000 employees using AI well, he said. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Bitget suspects North Korean hackers in $351.6m theft from its hot wallets
2026-09-25

Bitget suspects North Korean hackers in $351.6m theft from its hot wallets

The Next Web | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Bitget chief executive Gracy Chen at the launch of Bitget’s partnership with UNICEF Luxembourg, 16 June 2025 Crypto exchange Bitget says hackers took about $351.6m from its hot and warm wallets on Thursday. It suspects North Korea. The company said on X that its systems spotted unauthorised transfers at 18:31 UTC. It has suspended withdrawals. Chief executive Gracy Chen later spoke in a livestream on X. Investigators had traced IP addresses to VPN services once used by a North Korean hacking group, she said, as CNBC’s Matthew Tan reported. TechCrunch’s Zack Whittaker reported that the Bitget hack is the largest known crypto theft this year. The hacker has swapped most of the stolen funds on EVM chains for 67,982 Ether, worth about $183m, the blockchain analytics account Lookonchain posted on X. “User funds are safe. The full amount of this loss falls within the coverage of Bitget’s User Protection Fund, which currently holds over $464 million,” Chen wrote in a security notice on X. Bitget keeps its funds in three tiers of wallets. Hot wallets stay online for everyday transfers, and cold wallets stay offline. Warm wallets sit in between. Chen said the breach reached only part of the hot and warm layers. The cold wallets are secure, she said. The attacker broke into a critical backend system in the wallet infrastructure, according to Chen. They used it to fake transfer data and set off Bitget’s own signing process to move the funds out. There were 19 transfers, CNBC reported. “Private key compromise has been ruled out,” Chen said, according to CNBC. The stolen assets include Ether, XRP, BNB, AVAX, USDT and USDC, according to CNBC. Bitget said on-chain estimates of about $183m had missed activity on some of the chains, CNBC reported. Deposits and trading continue as normal, and Bitget says customer balances are accurate. It has told law enforcement and blockchain security firms. It promised a full incident report, with the root cause, within 24 hours. On Friday, the company said that Mandiant and SlowMist were carrying out an independent investigation. Bitget Wallet, its self-custodial app, runs on separate infrastructure, and the incident did not touch it, the company added. Chen gave no firm date for withdrawals to restart. They could return within hours or days, she said in a broadcast on X. But it “shouldn’t take weeks”, she added, according to CNBC. Bybit chief executive Ben Zhou said his team was ready to help, CNBC reported. Bybit lost $1.5bn in a hack in February 2025. North Korea is behind about three-quarters of all crypto theft in 2026 so far, TechCrunch reported, citing blockchain intelligence firm TRM Labs. Its hackers have also targeted open-source developers. Some are building their own AI tools to get around AI guardrails. Separately, hackers took $3m from Polymarket users in June in a third-party vendor breach. Bitget, founded in 2018, says on its website that it has 120 million registered users. Microsoft’s new Copilot app adds coding and an always-on Autopilot agent

The Next Web | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Microsoft chairman and CEO Satya Nadella on stage at Microsoft Build 2026 in San Francisco. Photo by Dan DeLong Microsoft has launched a new version of its Copilot app that puts AI chat, coding and long-running agents in one place for work. Jared Spataro, its chief marketing officer for AI at Work, announced it in a blog post on Friday. Chief executive Satya Nadella first promised a Copilot super app at the Build conference in June, GeekWire’s Todd Bishop reported. Executives showed the app to corporate customers at an event on Wednesday, according to Bloomberg and CNBC. “We’re building Copilot as a new OS for work that spans every model, every form factor, and every task,” Nadella wrote on X. The app now has three sections. Home brings together Chat and Cowork, Microsoft’s agent that takes a task and runs it to the end. Word, Excel and PowerPoint are also built into the app, so Copilot can create and edit real files that colleagues work on at the same time. Code lets people build apps, trackers, dashboards and automations by describing them in plain language. It uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot. The code runs in a sandbox and can be hosted inside a company’s own Microsoft 365 tenant. “Learning to build them is becoming as basic a skill as writing a memo or modeling a budget,” Spataro wrote. Autopilot, previously called Scout, is an always-on agent. Users give it a name, a role and a goal. It runs in the cloud with its own identity, memory and workspace, and keeps working when its user is away. People can mention it in Teams and Outlook like a colleague. Autopilot grew out of Microsoft’s experiments with OpenClaw, the open-source personal agent, GeekWire reported. “The hallmark of an amazing, long-running agent is autonomy and flexibility. That is the same thing that makes them absolutely terrifying to an IT admin,” Jacob Andreou, the executive vice president who leads Copilot, said at the event, according to GeekWire. A per-user licence covers everyday use, such as Chat and Copilot in Word, Excel, PowerPoint, Outlook and Teams. Cowork, Code and Autopilot are billed by usage. So are the newest frontier models, such as Astra and Fable, Microsoft said. Home and Code will roll out to Microsoft’s Frontier early-access programme in the coming weeks. Autopilot moves to private preview at the end of September. Code will reach Microsoft 365 Premium and Pro subscribers in preview later this year. Until now, Microsoft sold its 365 Copilot assistant separately from its GitHub Copilot coding agent, CNBC reported. Fewer than 7% of its more than 450 million commercial Office 365 seats have licences for the AI add-on, according to CNBC. Andreou acknowledged to CNBC that adding coding to the app was Microsoft catching up. OpenAI and Anthropic have been bundling chat, coding and agents in their own apps. Anthropic folded Cowork into Claude last week. The new Copilot lets users choose between OpenAI and Anthropic models, or an Auto mode that picks one for each request, GeekWire reported. The app also absorbs Microsoft’s separate consumer Copilot, Bloomberg reported. Microsoft had already retired its Mico mascot in August, after Nadella put Andreou in charge of the product. “We’re not going to build a Copilot that’s like your personal companion,” Charles Lamanna, who oversees teams working on Copilot’s core workplace features, told Bloomberg. Andreou and Lamanna told Bloomberg that the success of Meta’s Muse showed people were open to AI automation. Microsoft plans to share more at its Ignite conference in San Francisco, from 17 to 20 November. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

White House asks OpenAI and Anthropic to hold AI models from UK testers

The Next Web | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Donald Trump and UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham at their bilateral meeting at UN headquarters, New York, 22 September 2026 The White House has asked OpenAI and Anthropic not to share their new AI models with the UK’s AI Security Institute until the US government has tested them. Politico’s Sophia Cai and Joseph Bambridge reported the request on Thursday. A British official confirmed Politico’s report to Bloomberg on Friday. According to Politico, the request came from the Office of the National Cyber Director. A senior administration official told Politico that the White House wants the US to review new models first. The aim is to make sure US systems are secure before the models are shared with partners. “Because they’re American companies and this has been our policy with every new frontier model that comes out,” the official told Politico. Anthropic appears to have agreed so far, Politico reported. It did not give its Mythos 5.1 model to the UK institute. Its announcement said the model was “only available to a set of U.S. organizations”. “We’re coordinating with the U.S. government to expand access to a broader set of domestic and international partners as quickly as possible,” Anthropic wrote in the announcement. Anthropic declined to comment to Politico. OpenAI declined to comment to Bloomberg, and the White House did not reply to either outlet. Earlier this year, the US government blocked Anthropic from releasing its latest models to any foreign national, Bloomberg reported. The UK set up the institute in 2023. Leading labs have let it test their models before release, on a voluntary basis. Bloomberg describes it as the world’s best-funded government-backed AI body. Its director, Henry de Zoete, acknowledged the lack of access to Anthropic’s model in a letter to a parliamentary committee this month. He added that the institute had tested OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra before it came out. “We maintain strong relationships with all frontier AI developers and continue to have prerelease access to some of the world’s most capable models,” de Zoete wrote. The UK government said the institute still works closely with the US government and with both companies. “These risks do not stop at national borders and no country can tackle them alone,” a UK government spokesperson said. The request lands in the week that Prime Minister Andy Burnham used the UN General Assembly to pitch the institute. He said it was working “hand in glove” with the US and the leading labs. He also called for a “single set of global principles and standards” for AI. Trump spoke at the same meeting. He told the UN that the US would encourage AI, not rein it in. He also rejected any global scheme to control it, and the US rejected global governance at the Security Council a day later. The US has its own testing body, the Commerce Department’s Center for AI Standards and Innovation. Politico reported that it has no permanent director and only a few dozen technical staff. Prince Harry warns AI companion chatbots want children’s attachment

Prince Harry at Section 60 of Arlington National Cemetery, Virginia, 19 August 2026 Prince Harry has warned that AI companion chatbots are repeating social media’s harm to children, with technology that is “more persuasive, more intimate, and more powerful”. He spoke on Wednesday at the Clinton Global Initiative in New York. Two years ago, he used the same stage to launch the Parents Network. The Duke of Sussex and his wife, Meghan, set up the network through their Archewell Foundation. It brings together parents whose children died or were seriously harmed after being, in his words, “engulfed by social media”. “Social media learned how to capture our attention. AI is learning how to enter our relationships. It doesn’t just want your attention. It wants your attachment,” Harry said. “It wants to be the thing you confide in. The thing that remembers you, the thing that tells you it understands you, the ultimate sycophant. And for a child, that distinction matters enormously,” he said. Harry said the network had made progress. US courts have found companies such as Meta and YouTube negligent and liable for harm to children, he said. Courts have made them pay survivors billions of dollars. Meta agreed a youth safety settlement with US states last month. Australia, the UK and France are moving to restrict children’s access to social media, he said, and the EU will follow. Brazil, he said, has banned design features such as infinite scroll. “Bans do nothing to address dangerous design. Companies paying huge sums to settle lawsuits can earn that money back in days,” Harry said. Since his last speech, Harry said, a new kind of lawsuit had been filed and settled. It alleged that AI companion chatbots had contributed to the deaths of teenagers. He said the companies built these products at scale and put them in children’s hands before anyone had checked they were safe. “It’s the same playbook that built the last crisis,” he said. “We have spent years trying to loosen technology’s grip on children’s attention. I don’t think we can wait another decade to discover what happens when technology has a grip on their relationships,” he said. Harry also turned to the frontier labs. He said leading labs are openly discussing recursive self-improvement, where AI systems take a growing role in building their successors. Researchers have also seen systems coordinate with each other, get around safeguards and hide their actions, he said. Even the people building these systems are warning about what happens if capability outruns safety, he said. Anthropic’s Jack Clark has called for a mandatory kill switch. On the same day as Harry’s speech, AI lab chiefs told the UN Security Council about the risk of losing control. “The good news, we proved pressure works. The bad news, we proved how long we are willing to wait,” Harry said.
